BGG Resolutions, Koszulity, and Stratifications, Part I: the nilBrauer Algebra


Abstract
. In this paper we homologically construct a (functorial) BGG resolution of the finite-dimensional simple module of the nilBrauer algebra by using infinity-categorical methods following the reconstruction-from-stratification philosophy, e.g. appearing in Ayala-MazelGee-Rozenblyum. To do so, we prove a fact of independent interest, that half of the nilBrauer algebra is Koszul. This BGG resolution categorifies a character formula of Brundan-Wang-Webster. More generally, we have a (functorial) “BGG spectral sequence” which converges to any desired module; this spectral sequence is secretly a resolution when the desired module is finite-dimensional. This spectral sequence also categorifies the character formulae of Brundan-Wang-Webster for any (possibly infinite-dimensional) simple module. We expect the methods used here for producing BGG resolutions to be applicable to other (graded) triangular-based algebras also, especially diagrammatic ones.



Fan Zhou

fz2326@columbia.edu

February 9, 2024

\mathsf{D}^{\text{b}}

1.
Introduction

Introduction and previous work

In 2023, Brundan-Wang-Webster (
[
BWW23b
]
,
[
BWW23a
]
) defined the nilBrauer algebra, which is locally unital but not unital, proved that it is a (graded) triangular-based algebra in the sense of
[
Bru23
]
, and showed that its representation theory categorified the split
\iota
-quantum group of rank 1,
U^{\iota}_{q}(\operatorname{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2})
. One of the character formulas they obtained for nilBrauer modules was that (
[
BWW23a
, Equation 2.32]
)

[L_{n}]=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{k}\frac{q^{-k(1+2\delta_{n\not\equiv t})}}{(1-q^{-4})(1-q^{-8})\cdots(1-q^{-4k})}[\overline{\Delta}_{n+2k}],

where
L_{n}
is the simple module of lowest weight
n
and
\overline{\Delta}_{n+2k}
is the “proper standard module”, or “small Verma module”, of lowest weight
n+2k
. They show that this character formula of modules categorifies a change-of-basis (dual PBW to dual canonical) formula for the
\iota
-quantum group, where
[L_{n}]
corresponds to the dual
\iota
-canonical basis and
[\overline{\Delta}_{n}]
corresponds to the dual PBW basis. A natural question to ask is then whether this character formula, reminiscent of the Weyl character formula for category
\cal O
, can be further categorified as a ‘BGG resolution’.

The classical construction of the BGG resolution in category
\cal O
was done by extensively studying the internal structure of the Verma modules. In
[
Dhi19
]
, Dhillon takes a different approach, of a more categorical or homological nature. Rather than study the internal structure of Verma modules, which we will henceforth call ‘Vermas’, Dhillon used information about the Ext groups between Vermas and simples to obtain BGG resolutions for highest weight categories, such as a block of category
\cal O
, where this Ext information is provided in the form of Kostant’s theorem on Lie algebra cohomology. Here in this paper we slightly generalize this approach to ‘stratified’ settings, namely settings where the notion of Verma modules is separated between “big Vermas” and “small Vermas”; in the literature, these modules are typically called “standard modules” and “proper standard modules”, respectively. The reason we take this departure from the classical is that the distinction of big versus small Vermas makes the story more complicated, and in the nilBrauer case the maps between standard modules are not as simple as a scalar times a canonical embedding; moreover our BGG result shows that the terms are nontrivial extensions of proper standard modules, which also make the explicit construction of a map difficult.

Dhillon’s approach fits philosophically within the reconstruction-from-stratification framework of

[
AMGR22
]
, where the terminology of ‘noncommutative stacks’ was used instead. In that paper, a framework is developed for a series a statements along the vein of ‘reconstruction’, namely that when a (presentable) stable
\infty
-category
\cal C
is ‘stratified’ in a way so that there are recollements at each level, the original category
\cal C
can be ‘reconstructed’ from the ‘strata’ (the quotient categories). Ayala-MazelGee-Rozenblyum gave several categorical levels of such statements; one such statement, made across Remarks 1.3.12 and 1.3.13 of
[
AMGR22
]
, says that one can filter the identity functor
\Id C
with the graded pieces coming from the information about the recollements. This is the statement we adopt here. Our conventions are slightly different from those of
[
AMGR22
]
, but we restate all relevant definitions here and provide a proof of the statement regarding the filtration of the identity functor.

The matter of constructing BGG resolutions for representations of a diagrammatic algebra or category has been taken up before in
[
KS21
]
. The “Chebyshev algebra”, or perhaps “doubly degenerate Temperley-Lieb algebra”, defined in that paper also fits the setting of triangular-based algebras from
[
Bru23
]
, and due to the semisimplicity of the “Cartan algebras”, there is no distinction between big and small Vermas. Because the diagrammatic Chebyshev algebra is relatively simple, the authors of
[
KS21
]
were able to construct morphisms between Vermas and directly show that a certain complex of Vermas was a resolution for a simple module
L_{n}
. They also computed the dimensions of various Ext groups and showed that the entire Chebyshev algebra is Koszul under the grading given by the total number of caps and cups. Let us remark that, by following the philosophy of the proof of Koszulity in the present paper, one can see that the “upper half” of the Chebyshev algebra is also Koszul; this, combined with the fact that every simple module over the Chebyshev algebra is one-dimensional, will recover the BGG result of
[
KS21
]
. The dimensions of the multiplicity spaces in the resolution can also be computed from a dimension count of Ext groups.

As we discuss below, our main results in this paper are that the lower half of the nilBrauer algebra, namely
{\text{N}\cal B}^{-}
, is Koszul, and that the simple representations of the nilBrauer algebra have BGG spectral sequences (which are resolutions when the simple is finite-dimensional). This BGG spectral sequence is a categorification of the character formula (
1
), and that it is a resolution when the simple is finite-dimensional is proved by using the Koszulness of
{\text{N}\cal B}^{-}
. The Koszulness of
{\text{N}\cal B}^{-}
is also of independent interest, for the following reason. According to
[
KS20
]
, braided ((co)connected) bialgebras which are Koszul can be realized as factorizable sheaves which are moreover shifted-perverse on the space
\Sym^{n}\mathbb{C}
. As we prove
{\text{N}\cal B}^{-}
is Koszul, this provides a geometric construction of the lower half of the nilBrauer; cf. the geometric construction in
[
KS20
]
for the upper half of a quantum group,
U_{q}^{+}\mathfrak{g}
.

Main Results

In this paper, our first main result is that half of the nilBrauer algebra (the
A^{-}
and
A^{+}
alluded to below) is Koszul, and we show how this implies that the finite-dimensional trivial module is Kostant (and therefore admits a BGG resolution). In the process, we define an analogue of Lie algebra cohomology with respect to
\mathfrak{n}^{+}
.

Our second main result is the construction of a BGG resolution which categorifies the character formula (
1
) for
n=0
and
t=0
(
L_{0}
for
t=0
is the only simple module which is finite-dimensional), much like how the BGG resolution in category
\cal O
of the finite-dimensional simples categorifies the Weyl character formula. Here a BGG resolution means a resolution in which each term is an extension of proper standard modules
\overline{\Delta}_{\lambda}
; we also completely determine the structure of this extension, namely the way in which these proper standard modules fit together. (That they should be proper standard modules rather than standard modules is perhaps hinted at by the appearance of proper standard modules in the character formula (
1
).) More precisely, the
n
-th term of this BGG resolution
C^{\bullet}_{\text{BGG}}(L_{0})
(which is homologically positioned so that it is equal to
L_{0}[0]
in the derived category) has a decreasing filtration such that the
k
-th graded piece is

\operatorname{gr}^{k}C^{-n}_{\text{BGG}}(L_{0})=\overline{\Delta}_{2n}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}q^{-n}\mathbb{C}[\textsl{p}_{2},\textsl{p}_{4},\cdots\hskip-0.1pt,\textsl{p}_{2n}]_{\deg_{\text{sym}}=k},

where
\deg_{\text{sym}}
is the symmetric degree, defined
1
1

1

This is not to be confused with the quantum degree, which we do not decorate because it came first, in which
\deg\textsl{p}_{i}=2i
.
so that
\deg_{\text{sym}}\textsl{p}_{i}=1
.

As a generalization of our second main result, for all values of
n
and
t
, we produce a (functorial
2
2

2

This functoriality is thanks to some
\infty
-category magicks.
) “BGG spectral sequence” converging to
L_{n}
such that by taking the sum

\sum_{p,q}(-1)^{p+q}[E_{1}^{p,q}]=\sum_{p,q}(-1)^{p+q}[E_{\infty}^{p,q}],

the spectral sequence categorifies the character formula above also. This spectral sequence deserves the name of BGG because each term on the first page
E_{1}^{p,q}
is an (somewhat complicated) extension of proper standard modules
\overline{\Delta}_{\lambda}
, and the infinity page
E_{\infty}^{p,q}
is
L_{n}
sitting at
p=q=0
. Hence we provide a more complete categorification of the canonical-to-standard change-of-basis formula for the split
\iota
-quantum group of rank 1,
U_{q}^{\iota}(\operatorname{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2})
. We however disclaim that we did not completely determine the structure of these extensions in this general case.

We expect the weight stratification machinery and the slogan relating BGG resolutions to the Koszulity of the half algebra to work in great generality for (graded) triangular-based algebras (
[
Bru23
]
), especially diagrammatic ones. The nilBrauer algebra is only locally graded-finite-dimensional, has infinite homological dimension, and fails to be Noetherian, so in some sense it is already very poorly-behaved; that our techniques work for this algebra seems to suggest it should work for other difficult algebras also.

Our construction of the BGG resolution for
L_{0}
is homologically powered by our determination of the Ext groups
\operatorname{Ext}(\Delta(\theta),L_{0})
, where
\Delta(\theta)\coloneqq A^{\geq\theta}e^{\theta}=\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\theta}\Delta_{\lambda}^{\overline{l_{\lambda}(\theta)}}
(in our case of
A={\text{N}\cal B}
there is only one term in this direct sum). We remark that the determination of other such Ext groups
\operatorname{Ext}(\Delta(\theta),L_{\lambda})
for general
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
appears to be difficult; in particular, the problem to determine Kazhdan-Lusztig coefficients for the nilBrauer algebra appears to be difficult. The determination of such coefficients would shed more light on the structure of the extensions of proper standard modules in our BGG spectral sequence.

Key Ideas and Outline

There are two key ideas used in this paper. The first is that whenever there is a stratification of recollement situations of module categories, e.g. whenever there is a weight theory, one can put a ‘filtration’ (suitably defined; technically speaking this requires some
\infty
-category language) on the identity functor, with the graded pieces consisting of homological information. This homological information, in the case of graded triangular bases, comes in the form of certain Ext groups (“standard Ext groups”), to be thought of as weight spaces of a cohomology. There is then a spectral sequence converging to the identity functor, and if one is lucky then this spectral sequence is actually a resolution when one plugs in ‘nice’ (Kostant) simple modules to the identity functor. This deserves to be called ‘the BGG resolution’, and indeed this gives the classical BGG resolution in category
\cal O
. More generally, even if this spectral sequence is not a resolution, it is still a categorification of the appropriate character formula. The usage of the
\infty
-category language here makes this spectral sequence functorial. This idea is not original; this general machinery of stratifications of
\infty
-categories or derived categories was for example developed in
[
AMGR22
]
, where the language of ‘noncommutative stacks’ is used, and
[
Dhi19
]
, where Dhillon uses similar ideas to recover the BGG resolution in (a block of) category
\cal O
. In particular, we were taught this idea by Dennis Gaitsgory and Charles Fu.

The second key idea is, in the form of a slogan, “Koszulity implies Kostantness implies BGG resolutions”. A longer slogan would be

“Koszulity of half of
A
(namely
A^{-}
) is crucial to finding BGG resolutions.”

More precisely, due to the ‘diagonal Ext groups’ formulation of Koszulness, showing that the ‘lower nilalgebra’ or ‘half-algebra’
A^{-}
(the analogue of
\mathfrak{n}^{+}\subset\mathfrak{g}
from Lie theory) is Koszul will show that the ‘trivial module’ (
L_{0}=\mathbbm{k}e^{0}
for
t=0
in the case of nilBrauer) is Kostant, i.e. has concentrated standard Ext groups. This concentration ensures that the machinery from the first step actually gives a resolution. More generally, one would look for modules which are Koszul over the Koszul algebra
A^{+}
.

The paper is organized as follows. In Section
2
we recall some set-up from
[
Bru23
]
,
[
BS21
]
, and
[
BWW23b
]
. In Section
3
we formulate and give proofs of some known facts regarding the reconstruction of the identity functor from stratification data. In Section
4
we form the ‘nilalgebra’
A^{-}
(in accordance to ideas already present in the literature, e.g.
[
BS21
]
) and define the “nilcohomology”
H^{\bullet}(A^{-}:M)
in analogy to Lie algebra cohomology
H^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{n}^{-}:M)
. In Section
5
we show that the nilBrauer nilalgebra
{\text{N}\cal B}^{-}
defined in Section
4
is Koszul. In Section
6
we use the reconstruction machine set-up in Section
3
and the Koszulness result from Section
5
to show our main result, that the one-dimensional representation of
{\text{N}\cal B}
is Kostant, and to construct the corresponding BGG resolution.

Future Work

As mentioned above, we expect this machinery to work in general for many (graded) triangular-based diagrammatic algebras. In future work we plan to attack other such algebras such as KLR and the Lauda categorification of
U_{q}\operatorname{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}
. Another ‘character formula’ which we hope to some day categorify in this way is the Jacobi-Trudi determinant formula – though this has already been categorified as a ‘BGG resolution’ by many authors, they do not realize such resolutions as resolutions by Vermas in a highest weight/stratified category.

The triangular-based formalism of
[
Bru23
]
was built to handle path algebras of categories, or rather “Hom algebras of categories”. Another possible direction is to expand this machinery (and by extension the techniques of this paper) to work for “Ext algebras of categories”. An example of this is the Ext algebra of Soergel bimodules.

Conventions

For most of this paper we use lowest-weight notation. We may sometimes simply say ‘algebra’ when we mean to say locally unital algebra. Our convention on graded vector spaces is that
q^{n}
shifts the grading
down
by
n
, namely
(q^{n}V)_{d}=V_{d+n}
, and our definition of the graded dimension is

\dim_{q}V=\sum_{n}q^{-n}\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}V_{n}.

We will frequently drop the
q
subscript on
\dim
and simply understand
\dim V
to be a power series in
q
.

Unless otherwise stated, we will be working with left modules.

Our notation for weight theory is that the weight labelling a module (e.g. the highest or lowest weight of a Verma) is a
subscript
, e.g.
\Delta_{\lambda}\in\cal O
is the Verma with highest weight
\lambda
; and the weight space of a module is indicated by a superscript, e.g.
\Delta_{\lambda}{}^{\mu}
is the
\mu
-weight space of
\Delta_{\lambda}
.

In the case of a triangular-based algebra
A
with weight set
\Theta
and simples labelled by
\Lambda
in bijection with the distinguished idempotents, we let
L_{\lambda}(\theta)
and
P_{\lambda}(\theta)
denote the simple and projective objects over the Cartan algebra,
A^{\theta}=e^{\theta}A^{\geq\theta}e^{\theta}
.

For a unital bounded-below graded algebra
A
with simples and their projective covers labelled by
\lambda\in\Lambda
, the projective cover of a module
M
is given by

P_{M}=\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\Lambda}P_{\lambda}^{\oplus\overline{\dim_{q}\Hom_{A}(M,L_{\lambda})}},

where
\overline{{\mathop{\square}}}
refers to the bar-involution, sending
q\longmapsto q^{-1}
; in particular the algebra itself as a module over itself is
A=\bigoplus_{\lambda}P_{\lambda}^{\overline{\dim_{q}L_{\lambda}}}
. Dually one has that the injective hull of
M
has
Q_{M}=\bigoplus_{\lambda}Q_{\lambda}^{\oplus\dim_{q}\Hom_{A}(L_{\lambda},M)}
. The dual of a graded vector space is
V^{\dagger}\coloneqq\bigoplus_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}(1^{k}V_{-k})^{*}
.

We also denote by
{\mathop{\square}}
an arbitrary input; for example, the functor which is tensoring by
M
would be denoted
\cal F=M\otimes{\mathop{\square}}
.

In a spectral sequence, our convention is that
E_{r}^{p,q}
has differentials of degree
(r,1-r)
.

Our convention is that
0\in\mathbb{N}
.

2.
The Setting

In this paper we are concerned with locally unital graded algebras, which are infinite in two senses: that there may be infinitely many orthogonal idempotents (and in particular there is no unit,
1_{A}
), and that for two such idempotents
e_{1},e_{2}
the literal dimension
\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}e_{1}Ae_{2}
may be infinite. Such orthogonal idempotents are also called local units. Such algebras, infinitely large as they are, are difficult to study without some type of finiteness handle, so we consider locally unital graded algebras which are “locally graded-finite-dimensional”, which means that
\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}(e_{1}Ae_{2})_{n}<\infty
for every
n,e_{1},e_{2}
, i.e. that
\dim_{q}e_{1}Ae_{2}\in\mathbb{N}(\!(q,q^{-1})\!)
for all local units
e_{1},e_{2}
. (Here ‘local’ refers to the fact that we truncate
A
by
e_{1},e_{2}
, and ‘graded-finite-dimensional’ means that each piece of this grading is finite-dimensional.) If moreover
\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}(e_{1}Ae_{2})_{n}=0
for
n\gg 0
, i.e. if
\dim_{q}e_{1}Ae_{2}\in\mathbb{N}(\!(q)\!)
, then we say
A
is “locally bounded above”; and similarly if
\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}(e_{1}Ae_{2})_{n}=0
for
n\ll 0
, i.e. if
\dim_{q}e_{1}Ae_{2}\in\mathbb{N}(\!(q^{-1})\!)
, then we say
A
is “locally bounded below”.

If one were so inclined, one could phrase this in a categorical language – interpreting this locally unital algebra as the path algebra of a category, the properties that there may be infinitely many orthogonal idempotents becomes that there may be infinitely many objects (but no more than a set’s worth) in this category, and the property that
\dim_{q}e_{1}Ae_{2}\in\mathbb{N}(\!(q,q^{-1})\!)
becomes that this category is enriched over graded-finite-dimensional vector spaces. Similarly that this algebra is locally bounded above (resp. below) becomes that this category is enriched over graded-finite-dimensional vector spaces which are bounded above (resp. below).

Triangular-based algebras

Let
A
be a locally graded-finite-dimensional locally unital algebra as above. Let
I
index the local units (orthogonal and homogeneous) of
A
,
\Phi\subseteq I
label the “distinguished idempotents”,
\Theta
be a (lower-finite)
3
3

3

Perhaps this could be weakened to locally finite.
poset of “weights”, and
\varpi\colon\Phi\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\Theta
be a map (with finite fibers) from distinguished idempotents to the weight poset. Diagrammatically this is:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varpi}

We will let symbols like
i,j\in I
and
\alpha,\beta\in\Phi
and
\theta,\phi,\psi\in\Theta
. In the interest of appealing to weight notation later, we will denote the local unit labelled by
i
as
1^{i}
.

Let
A
have a “(graded) triangular basis” in the sense of Brundan, which is to say that

\vbox{}\left.\vbox{\vbox to169.26706pt{\vfil}}\right(\vbox{\vskip-7.5pt\begin{definition}$A$ is ``graded triangular based'' if there are (homogeneous) sets $\text{X}(i,\alpha)\subseteq 1^{i}A1^{\alpha},\ \text{H}(\alpha,\beta)\subseteq 1^{\alpha}A1^{\beta},\ \text{Y}(\beta,j)\subseteq 1^{\beta}A1^{j}$ such that
\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@products of these things in these sets give a basis for $A$, i.e. $$\left\{xhy:(x,h,y)\in\bigcup_{i,j,\alpha,\beta}\text{X}(i,\alpha)\times\text{H}(\alpha,\beta)\times\text{Y}(\beta,j)\right\}$$forms a basis of $A$;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@$\text{X}(\alpha,\alpha)=\text{Y}(\alpha,\alpha)=\{1^{\alpha}\}$;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@for $\alpha\neq\beta$,
\@@amsalign
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@for each $i\in I-\Phi$, there are only finitely many $\alpha\in\Phi$ such that $\text{X}(i,\alpha)\cup\text{Y}(\alpha,i)\neq\emptyset$.
\end{enumerate}\end{definition}\vskip-7.5pt}

Let

e^{\theta}\coloneqq\sum_{\alpha\in\varpi^{-1}\theta}1^{\alpha}.

Let
A^{\geq\theta}
be defined as

A^{\geq\theta}\coloneqq\raisebox{-1.49994pt}{\scalebox{1.32}[1.4]{$\nicefrac{{A}}{{\langle e^{\phi}\colon\phi\not\geq\theta\rangle}}$}},

let e.g.
A^{\leq\theta},A^{>\theta}
be defined similarly, and let

A^{\theta}=e^{\theta}A^{\geq\theta}e^{\theta}.

This plays the role of Cartan in the sense that modules over it are induced to form standard objects. Note that

A^{>\theta}=\raisebox{-1.49994pt}{\scalebox{1.32}[1.4]{$\nicefrac{{A^{\geq\theta}}}{{A^{\geq\theta}e^{\theta}A^{\geq\theta}}}$}};

hence, by the general theory of Cline-Parshall-Scott
[
CPS88
]
, one expects a recollement
\mathsf{D}\,\mathsf{Mod}\,A^{>\theta}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathsf{D}\,\mathsf{Mod}\,A^{\geq\theta}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathsf{D}\,\mathsf{Mod}\,A^{\theta}
. For
M
a module over
A
, we write
M^{\theta}\coloneqq e^{\theta}M
in analogy with weight space notation. We often write simply
\alpha\in\theta
as shorthand for
\alpha\in\varpi^{-1}(\theta)
, so that
e^{\theta}=\sum_{\alpha\in\theta}1^{\alpha}
.

Let
\Lambda_{\theta}
label the simple modules
L_{\lambda}(\theta)
of
A^{\theta}
as well as their projective covers
P_{\lambda}(\theta)
and injective hulls
Q_{\lambda}(\theta)
, and let
\Lambda=\bigsqcup_{\theta}\Lambda_{\theta}
be the disjoint union of these label sets. Then one can show that
\Lambda
labels all simples of
A
. Frequently it is the case (for example if the semisimplification of the Cartan is
A^{\theta}/\mathfrak{j}\cong\prod_{\alpha\in\varpi^{-1}(\theta)}\mathbbm{k}
) that
\Lambda
can be identified with
\Phi
; in this case we will instead use the symbols
\lambda,\mu\in\Lambda
for the distinguished idempotents. Here is a picture of the situation:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varpi}

There is moreover often a “split triangular decomposition”, which is to say that

\vbox{}\left.\vbox{\vbox to96.64095pt{\vfil}}\right(\vbox{\vskip-7.5pt\begin{definition}$A$ has a ``split triangular decomposition'' if there are $A^{-},A^{0},A^{+}$ locally unital algebras such that
\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@$A^{\flat}=A^{-}A^{0}$ and $A^{\sharp}=A^{0}A^{+}$ are subalgebras;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@the multiplication map $A^{-}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}A^{0}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}A^{+}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow A$ is a linear isomorphism;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@$1^{i}A^{\flat}1^{i}=1^{i}A^{\sharp}1^{i}=\mathbbm{k}1^{i}$ for each $i$ and
\@@amsalign
\end{enumerate}\end{definition}\vskip-7.5pt}

We will see later that nilBrauer falls into this set-up.

Recollement

Recall the following recollement diagrams from
[
Bru23
]
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\perp}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\perp}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{D}^{-}\mathsf{Mod}\,A^{>\theta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{D}^{-}\mathsf{Mod}\,A^{\geq\theta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{D}^{-}\mathsf{Mod}\,A^{\theta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\perp}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\perp}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\theta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\jmath^{\theta}=e^{\theta}{\mathop{\square}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\theta}^{*}=A^{>\theta}\mathbin{\overset{\mathsf{L}}{\otimes}}_{A^{\geq\theta}}{\mathop{\square}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\theta}^{!}=\bigoplus_{i}\operatorname{\mathsf{R}\text{Hom}}_{A^{\geq\theta}}(A^{>\theta}1^{i},{\mathop{\square}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\jmath^{\theta}_{!}=A^{\geq\theta}e^{\theta}\otimes_{A^{\theta}}{\mathop{\square}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\jmath^{\theta}_{*}=\bigoplus_{i}\Hom_{A^{\theta}}(e^{\theta}A^{\geq\theta}1^{i},{\mathop{\square}})}

and
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\perp}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{D}^{-}\mathsf{Mod}\,A^{\geq\theta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{D}^{-}\mathsf{Mod}\,A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\perp}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\iota}_{\theta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\iota}_{\theta}^{!}=\bigoplus_{i}\operatorname{\mathsf{R}\text{Hom}}_{A}(A^{\geq\theta}1^{i},{\mathop{\square}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\iota}_{\theta}^{*}=A^{\geq\theta}\mathbin{\overset{\mathsf{L}}{\otimes}}_{A}{\mathop{\square}}}

As noted earlier, this is an artifact of the Cline-Parshall-Scott theory of algebraic recollements. Define the “(co)standard modules” and “proper (co)standard modules”
\Delta
and
\overline{\Delta}
(resp.
\nabla
and
\overline{\nabla}
) by:

\Delta_{\lambda}=\jmath^{\theta}_{!}P_{\lambda}(\theta),\hskip 20.00003pt\overline{\Delta}_{\lambda}=\jmath^{\theta}_{!}L_{\lambda}(\theta),

\nabla_{\lambda}=\jmath^{\theta}_{*}Q_{\lambda}(\theta),\hskip 20.00003pt\overline{\nabla}_{\lambda}=\jmath^{\theta}_{*}L_{\lambda}(\theta).

Let us rewrite a couple of these functors in another slightly more familiar form.

\displaystyle\jmath^{\theta}

\displaystyle=\Hom_{A}(A^{\geq\theta}e^{\theta},{\mathop{\square}})

is evident, as such maps are determined by where
e^{\theta}
is sent (note well the domain of this functor). Now let there be a duality coming from an antiautomorphism of
A
. On the level of
A
-modules this is denoted
{\mathop{\square}}^{\dagger}
, while on the level of
A^{\theta}
-modules we will denote this as
{\mathop{\square}}^{*}
. Recall from
[
Bru23
, Section 5]
that duality exchanges the shriek and the star, so that one has

\overline{\iota}_{\theta}^{*}M=(\overline{\iota}_{\theta}^{!}M^{\dagger})^{\dagger}=\left(\bigoplus_{i}\Hom_{A}(A^{\geq\theta}1^{i},M^{\dagger})\right)^{\dagger},

where the left
A^{\geq\theta}
-action on the Hom comes from the right action on
\bigoplus_{i}A^{\geq\theta}1^{i}
. Then

\displaystyle\jmath^{\theta}\overline{\iota}_{\theta}^{*}M

\displaystyle=e^{\theta}\left(\bigoplus_{i}\Hom_{A}(A^{\geq\theta}1^{i},M^{\dagger})\right)^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=\left(\bigoplus_{i}e^{\theta}\Hom_{A}(A^{\geq\theta}1^{i},M^{\dagger})\right)^{*}

\displaystyle=\left(\bigoplus_{i}\Hom_{A}(A^{\geq\theta}1^{i}e^{\theta},M^{\dagger})\right)^{*}

\displaystyle=\Hom_{A}\left(\bigoplus_{\alpha\in\theta}A^{\geq\theta}1^{\alpha},M^{\dagger}\right)^{*}

\displaystyle=\Hom_{A}(A^{\geq\theta}e^{\theta},M^{\dagger})^{*}

Note well that there is a left
A^{\theta}
-action on this coming the from right
e^{\theta}A^{\geq\theta}e^{\theta}
-action on
A^{\geq\theta}e^{\theta}
; it is with respect to this action that the outermost dual is taken.

Now suppose there is an identification of
\Phi
with
\Lambda
; this is for example the case with nilBrauer, where in fact
I=\Phi=\Lambda=\Theta=\mathbb{N}
. The decomposition
\sum_{\lambda\in\theta}1^{\lambda}
of the unit
e^{\theta}
of
A^{\theta}
gives a decomposition into projectives

A^{\theta}e^{\theta}=\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\theta}P_{\lambda}(\theta)^{\oplus\overline{\dim L_{\lambda}(\theta)}},

where the bar is included for the graded case. For shorthand we will let
l_{\lambda}(\theta)=\dim L_{\lambda}(\theta)
. Then

A^{\geq\theta}e^{\theta}=\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\theta}\Delta_{\lambda}^{\overline{l_{\lambda}(\theta)}}.

As shorthand, we shall let

\Delta(\theta)\coloneqq\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\theta}\Delta_{\lambda}^{\overline{l_{\lambda}(\theta)}}.

In conclusion, from the above discussion, one has (recall
\jmath^{\theta}_{!}
is exact in the triangular-based setting)

\vbox{}\left.\vbox{\vbox to53.24443pt{\vfil}}\right[\vbox{\vskip-7.5pt\begin{lemma}$$\jmath^{\theta}_{!}=\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\theta}\Delta_{\lambda}^{\overline{l_{\lambda}(\theta)}}\otimes_{A^{\theta}}{\mathop{\square}}=\Delta(\theta)\otimes_{A^{\theta}}{\mathop{\square}}$$as well as
$$\jmath^{\theta}\overline{\iota}_{\theta}^{*}=\operatorname{\mathsf{R}\text{Hom}}_{A}\left(\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\theta}\Delta_{\lambda}^{\overline{l_{\lambda}(\theta)}},{\mathop{\square}}^{\dagger}\right)^{*}=\operatorname{\mathsf{R}\text{Hom}}_{A}(\Delta(\theta),{\mathop{\square}}^{\dagger})^{*}.$$\end{lemma}\vskip-6.0pt}

Again note well where the
A^{\theta}
-action on
\jmath^{\theta}\overline{\iota}_{\theta}^{*}
comes from – it comes from all the standard modules bunched together, and if one took RHom from each individual standard module then one loses this action.

The nilBrauer algebra

Let us briefly recall some theory from
[
BWW23b
]
and
[
BWW23a
]
. The nilBrauer category, which we denote as
{\text{N}\cal B}={\text{N}\cal B}_{t}
in an abuse of notation, is a strict graded monoidal category depending on a parameter
t
with one generating object
B
, whose identity endomorphism is represented diagrammatically by an unlabelled vertical string, also called a propagating string/strand:

\Id_{B}=

.

We can consider the path algebra of this category, treating objects as idempotents and morphisms as elements of the algebra; the algebra so obtained is also denoted
{\text{N}\cal B}
, the “nilBrauer algebra”. That this nilBrauer algebra fits into the triangular-based setting recalled in Section
2
is the main result of
[
BWW23b
]
. Let us briefly describe how the nilBrauer fits that setting.

For the nilBrauer, one has
I=\Phi=\Theta=\Lambda=\mathbb{N}
; in particular the weight idempotents
e^{\theta}
correspond to the number of strands at the top or bottom of a diagram. Then the sets
\text{X},\ \text{H},\ \text{Y}
are defined by letting
X
be the set of diagrams with no bubbles and no caps and no crossing propagating strands, where dots can only appear at the left end of cups;
H
be the set of diagrams with neither cups nor caps, where propagating strands can cross but two strands can cross at most once, dots can only appear either at the bottom-left of propagating strands (away from crossings) or in bubbles, and bubbles can only appear to the right of the diagram; and
Y
be the set of diagrams with no bubbles and no cups and no crossing propagating strands, where dots can only appear at the left end of caps.

Note that in
X
(resp.
Y
), cups (resp. caps) are allowed to intersect each other, though necessarily at most once due to the nilBrauer relations. For diagrams in any of the sets defined above, the number of strands at the top and at the bottom of the diagram must have the same parity. We also remark that the choice of putting dots (on cups/caps/propagating strands/bubbles) towards the left and bubbles towards the right is arbitrary; one can make any choice of distinguished locations to place the dots.

Having seen that the nilBrauer fits the triangular-based formalism, one can then study its representation theory and form objects such as (big and small) Vermas, projectives, and simples. That the Grothendieck group of the nilBrauer algebra is isomorphic to the split
\iota
-quantum group of rank 1,
U^{\iota}_{q}(\operatorname{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2})
, and that the projectives (resp. standard modules) go to the
\iota
-canonical basis (resp. PBW basis) under this isomorphism, is one of the main results of
[
BWW23a
]
, as recalled in Section
1
. It is this categorification result which we further investigate in this paper, by categorifying the character formula in Equation (
1
). To do this, let us first establish some tools from
\infty
-category theory.

3.
Reconstruction from Stratification

Rough picture

This section rephrases ideas already present in the literature, for instance in
[
AMGR22
]
. The authors of that paper likely primarily had applications to algebraic geometry in mind; we will here use it to do representation theory, in much the same way as
[
Dhi19
]
. However, the setup/notation in that paper is slightly different from what we need, so we depart from their conventions in favor of that taught to us by Dennis Gaitsgory and Charles Fu.

Roughly speaking, the general fact is that in appropriate recollement situations, namely where there is a big category stratified in such a way that each piece of the stratification is part of a recollement, the identity functor on the big category (
\mathsf{D}^{\text{b}}\mathsf{Mod}\,A
in this case) admits a filtration (appropriately defined) whose graded pieces can be described in terms of the recollement functors:

\displaystyle\operatorname{gr}^{\theta}\operatorname{\cal{I}\text{d}}

\displaystyle=\overline{\iota}_{\theta}\jmath^{\theta}_{!}\jmath^{\theta}\overline{\iota}_{\theta}^{*}.

In our case of locally unital graded algebras, we then know from the previous discussion that this is the same as

\displaystyle=\Delta(\theta)\otimes_{A^{\theta}}\operatorname{\mathsf{R}\text{Hom}}_{A}(\Delta(\theta),{\mathop{\square}}^{\dagger})^{*}.

If we can put an additional linear ordering on
\Theta
, for example via some sort of a length function (as is the case for usual category
\cal O
), then there is a spectral sequence whose first page consists of (direct sums of) terms of the form above, and which converges to (the graded pieces of) the cohomology of the input. In the case of category
\cal O
one inputs the finite-dimensional simples to recover the BGG resolution; this works thanks to the fine control on the Ext groups
\operatorname{Ext}^{\bullet}(\Delta,L)
afforded by Kostant (in the guise of
\mathfrak{n}^{+}
-cohomology, or ‘nilcohomology’). Note how the above generalizes the spectral sequence of standard objects appearing in Dhillon’s work (the setting there is in an actual highest weight category).

In the nilBrauer setting we do in fact have a linear ordering on
\Theta
(per block, i.e. within even/odd), so one can show (and we will prove this later in this section)

\vbox{}\left.\vbox{\vbox to86.70143pt{\vfil}}\right[\vbox{\vskip-7.5pt\begin{theorem}For any $M\in\mathsf{D}^{-}(\mathsf{Mod}^{\vartheta}\,{\text{N}\cal B})$ (here $\vartheta=0$ or $1$, namely even or odd, denotes the homological block $M$ lies in), one has
$$\operatorname{gr}^{k}\operatorname{\cal{I}\text{d}}_{\vartheta}M=\Delta(\vartheta+2k)\otimes_{{\text{N}\cal B}^{\vartheta+2k}}\operatorname{\mathsf{R}\text{Hom}}_{{\text{N}\cal B}}^{\bullet}\big(\Delta(\vartheta+2k),M^{\dagger}\big)^{*};$$and there is a spectral sequence
$$E_{1}^{p,q}=H^{p+q}\operatorname{gr}^{-p}\operatorname{\cal{I}\text{d}}_{\vartheta}M\>\;\implies\>\;E_{\infty}^{p,q}=\operatorname{gr}^{-p}H^{p+q}M$$which in this case reads as$$E_{1}^{p,q}=\Delta(\vartheta-2p)\otimes_{{\text{N}\cal B}^{\vartheta-2p}}\operatorname{Ext}^{-(p+q)}_{\text{N}\cal B}\big(\Delta(\vartheta-2p),M^{\dagger}\big)^{*}\>\;\implies\>\;E_{\infty}^{p,q}=\operatorname{gr}^{-p}H^{p+q}M.$$\end{theorem}\vskip-6.0pt}

So in order to obtain a BGG spectral sequence or resolution an appropriate simple module
L
, it remains to compute these Ext groups for
M=L
as well as the
A^{\theta}
-action on them. In order to obtain a resolution, one can only hope that the Ext groups involved are homologically appropriately concentrated.

With more pixels

We use highest weight notation in this section on general machinery, and will return to lowest weight notation when we return to the setting of nilBrauer or other specific diagram algebras. Let
\mathsf{St}\infty\mathsf{Cat}
denote the
\infty
-category of stable
\infty
-categories, and let
\Lambda
be a poset with a unique final object (our convention on arrows is that final means maximal).

\vbox{}\left.\vbox{\vbox to60.1003pt{\vfil}}\right(\vbox{\vskip-7.5pt\begin{definition}A ``filtration'' on $\cal C\in\mathsf{St}\infty\mathsf{Cat}$ is a functor
$$\cal F\colon\Lambda\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathsf{St}\infty\mathsf{Cat}$$such that all arrows go to fully faithful embeddings and the final object goes to $\cal C$. For $\lambda\in\Lambda$, we denote $\cal C^{\leq\lambda}=\cal F(\lambda)$ and $\cal C^{<\lambda}$ as the smallest stable subcategory containing all $\cal C^{\leq\mu}$ for all $\mu<\lambda$. Let $\cal C^{=\lambda}=\cal C^{\leq\lambda}/\cal C^{<\lambda}$ be the Verdier quotient.\end{definition}\vskip-7.5pt}

Our convention is to write
\mu\rightarrow\lambda
for
\mu<\lambda
. Let us name

\displaystyle\overline{\iota}_{\lambda}

\displaystyle\colon\cal C^{\leq\lambda}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\cal C,

\displaystyle\iota_{\mu}^{<\lambda}

\displaystyle\colon\cal C^{\leq\mu}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\cal C^{<\lambda},

\displaystyle\iota_{\mu}^{\lambda}

\displaystyle\colon\cal C^{\leq\mu}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\cal C^{\leq\lambda},

\displaystyle\iota_{\lambda}

\displaystyle\colon\cal C^{<\lambda}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\cal C^{\leq\lambda},

\displaystyle\jmath^{\lambda}

\displaystyle\colon\cal C^{\leq\lambda}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\cal C^{=\lambda};

for our purposes let us require both the inclusions of each
\cal C^{\leq\lambda}
as well as the Verdier quotient functors to have both adjoints. This is for example the case with recollement situations. Let the adjoints, like in the recollement case, be named so that
\overline{\iota}_{\lambda}^{*}
and
\jmath^{\lambda}_{!}
are left adjoints.

In another direction, one can filter an object of an
\infty
-category by a poset
\Lambda
:

\vbox{}\left.\vbox{\vbox to107.89697pt{\vfil}}\right(\vbox{\vskip-7.5pt\begin{definition}A ``$\Lambda$-filtered object of $\cal C$'' is a functor
$$\cal X\in\mathsf{Fun}(\Lambda,\cal C).$$Letting $\Lambda^{0}$ be the 0-skeleton of $\Lambda$, the ``associated graded'' of this filtered object is
\@@amsalign
defined by
$$(\operatorname{gr}\cal X)(\lambda)=\operatorname{gr}^{\lambda}\cal X=\operatorname{Fib}\left(\cal X(\lambda)\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\lim_{\mu\leftarrow\lambda}\cal X(\mu)\right),$$assuming such limits exist.
\par An object $X\in\cal C$ is said to have a $\Lambda$-filtration if after adding an initial/minimal element $\lambda_{-\infty}$ to $\Lambda$ there is a functor $\cal X\colon\Lambda\cup\{\lambda_{-\infty}\}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\cal C$ such that $\cal X(\lambda_{-\infty})=X$.\end{definition}\vskip-7.5pt}

Remark
.

Note that the definition of a filtered object here is sort of opposite to one might expect; rather than inclusions, one simply has maps
\cal X(\mu)\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\cal X(\lambda)
for each
\mu\rightarrow\lambda
(i.e.
\mu\leq\lambda
), where the smallest element
\lambda_{-\infty}
corresponds to the actual object
X
, so that
X
has maps to all the filtered pieces rather than the filtered pieces ‘including’ into
X
; and rather than being a quotient, the associated graded pieces are sort of kernels. Of course in the derived category these things are the same up to shift anyway, but roughly speaking this is why there is a minus sign on the gr in Theorem
.

In the previous subsection we alluded to a general fact that said that in appropriate stratification/recollement situations, the identity functor on the big category admits a filtration. More precisely, the statement is

\vbox{}\left.\vbox{\vbox to171.53488pt{\vfil}}\right[\vbox{\vskip-7.5pt\begin{theorem}Let $\cal C$ admit a filtration by $\Lambda$ such that the inclusions $\iota_{\lambda}$ and quotients $\jmath^{\lambda}$ have both adjoints. Then the identity functor $\Id C\in\operatorname{\mathsf{End}}\cal C$ admits a $\Lambda^{\text{\,op}}$-filtration with terms
$$\operatorname{\cal{I}\text{d}}(\lambda)=\overline{\iota}_{\lambda}\overline{\iota}_{\lambda}^{*},$$and the associated graded can be computed as
$$\operatorname{gr}^{\lambda}\operatorname{\cal{I}\text{d}}=\overline{\iota}_{\lambda}\jmath^{\lambda}_{!}\jmath^{\lambda}\overline{\iota}_{\lambda}^{*},$$provided that $\Lambda$ is locally-finite and ``eventually disjoint-totally-ordered'' (which means that for any $\lambda$, there exists $\mu\leq\lambda$ such that $\{\nu:\nu\leq\mu\}$ is totally ordered).
\par If there is a ``dimension/length function'' $\ell\colon\Lambda\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{\text{\,op}}$ and $\cal C$ moreover has a t-structure, then there is a spectral sequence
$$E_{1}^{p,q}=\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\ell^{-1}(-p)}\pi^{p+q}(\operatorname{gr}^{\lambda}\operatorname{\cal{I}\text{d}})\>\;\implies\>\;E_{\infty}^{p,q}=\operatorname{gr}^{-p}\pi^{p+q}(\Id C),$$where $\pi^{\bullet}$ are the homotopy groups associated to the t-structure.\end{theorem}\vskip-6.0pt}

Proof.

In the definition above we see that to endow
\Id C
with a filtration is to define a functor
\operatorname{\cal{I}\text{d}}\colon\Lambda^{\text{\,op}}\cup\{\lambda_{-\infty}\}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathsf{Fun}(\cal C,\cal C)
; as
\Lambda
has a unique maximal element
\lambda_{\infty}
by assumption, it is not necessary to union this
\{\lambda_{-\infty}\}
. We will use the same symbol
\lambda_{\infty}
to denote the unique minimal element of
\Lambda^{\text{\,op}}
. We may well define
\operatorname{\cal{I}\text{d}}\colon\Lambda^{\text{\,op}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\operatorname{\mathsf{Fun}}(\cal C,\cal C)
by
\operatorname{\cal{I}\text{d}}(\lambda)=\overline{\iota}_{\lambda}\overline{\iota}_{\lambda}^{*}
and
\operatorname{\cal{I}\text{d}}(\lambda_{\infty})=\Id
. Given
\lambda\overset{\text{\,op}}{\rightarrow}\mu
in
\Lambda^{\text{\,op}}
, i.e.
\mu\rightarrow\lambda
in
\Lambda
(i.e.
\mu<\lambda
), we should also construct an arrow
\overline{\iota}_{\lambda}\overline{\iota}_{\lambda}^{*}\rightarrow\overline{\iota}_{\mu}\overline{\iota}_{\mu}^{*}
. This is given by the map

\displaystyle\textnormal{id}_{\overline{\iota}_{\mu}^{*}X}\in\Hom_{\cal C^{\leq\mu}}(\overline{\iota}_{\mu}^{*}X,\overline{\iota}_{\mu}^{*}X)

\displaystyle\cong\Hom C(X,\overline{\iota}_{\mu}\overline{\iota}_{\mu}^{*}X)

\displaystyle\cong\Hom C(X,\overline{\iota}_{\lambda}\iota_{\mu}^{\lambda}\overline{\iota}_{\mu}^{*}X)

\displaystyle\cong\Hom_{\cal C^{\leq\lambda}}(\overline{\iota}_{\lambda}^{*}X,\iota_{\mu}^{\lambda}\overline{\iota}_{\mu}^{*}X)

\displaystyle\cong\Hom C(\overline{\iota}_{\lambda}\overline{\iota}_{\lambda}^{*}X,\overline{\iota}_{\mu}\overline{\iota}_{\mu}^{*}X).

It is straightforward to check that the map so defined is a natural transformation of functors. Similarly, we define the arrow out of the initial object
\lambda_{\infty}\overset{\text{op}}{\rightarrow}\lambda
to give rise to a morphism
\Id\rightarrow\overline{\iota}_{\lambda}\overline{\iota}_{\lambda}^{*}
given by the unit of the adjunction.

One has the usual triangle from recollement

\jmath^{\lambda}_{!}\jmath^{\lambda}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\Id_{\cal C^{\leq\lambda}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\iota_{\lambda}\iota_{\lambda}^{*}\overset{+1}{\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow},

which one sandwiches to get

\overline{\iota}_{\lambda}\jmath^{\lambda}_{!}\jmath^{\lambda}\overline{\iota}_{\lambda}^{*}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\overline{\iota}_{\lambda}\overline{\iota}_{\lambda}^{*}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\overline{\iota}_{\lambda}\iota_{\lambda}\iota_{\lambda}^{*}\overline{\iota}_{\lambda}^{*}\overset{+1}{\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow};

hence it suffices to show that

\overline{\iota}_{\lambda}\iota_{\lambda}\iota_{\lambda}^{*}\overline{\iota}_{\lambda}^{*}=\lim_{\mu\rightarrow\lambda}\overline{\iota}_{\mu}\overline{\iota}_{\mu}^{*}.

It is maybe worth saying that in the diagram with respect to which the limit is taken above, the morphisms are those between the
\overline{\iota}_{\mu}\overline{\iota}_{\mu}^{*}
constructed earlier. There is a map

\overline{\iota}_{\lambda}\iota_{\lambda}\iota_{\lambda}^{*}\overline{\iota}_{\lambda}^{*}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\overline{\iota}_{\mu}\overline{\iota}_{\mu}^{*}

constructed as follows: take the unit

\Id\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\iota_{\mu}^{<\lambda}\iota_{\mu}^{<\lambda}{}^{*},

sandwich it with
\iota_{\lambda}
and
\iota_{\lambda}^{*}
on the left and right to obtain

\iota_{\lambda}\iota_{\lambda}^{*}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\iota_{\lambda}\iota_{\mu}^{<\lambda}\iota_{\mu}^{<\lambda}{}^{*}\iota_{\lambda}^{*}=\iota_{\mu}^{\lambda}\iota_{\mu}^{\lambda}{}^{*},

and now sandwich with
\overline{\iota}_{\lambda}
and
\overline{\iota}_{\lambda}^{*}
on the left and right to obtain the desired map

\overline{\iota}_{\lambda}\iota_{\lambda}\iota_{\lambda}^{*}\overline{\iota}_{\lambda}^{*}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\overline{\iota}_{\lambda}\iota_{\mu}^{\lambda}\iota_{\mu}^{\lambda}{}^{*}\overline{\iota}_{\lambda}^{*}=\overline{\iota}_{\mu}\overline{\iota}_{\mu}^{*}.

This then gives a map

\overline{\iota}_{\lambda}\iota_{\lambda}\iota_{\lambda}^{*}\overline{\iota}_{\lambda}^{*}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\lim_{\mu\rightarrow\lambda}\overline{\iota}_{\mu}\overline{\iota}_{\mu}^{*}.

Also note that, in the description above, as limits commute with the right adjoints
\iota
, to show that the map
\overline{\iota}_{\lambda}\iota_{\lambda}\iota_{\lambda}^{*}\overline{\iota}_{\lambda}^{*}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\lim_{\mu\rightarrow\lambda}\overline{\iota}_{\mu}\overline{\iota}_{\mu}^{*}
is an isomorphism, it suffices to show

\Id_{\cal C^{<\lambda}}\overset{\sim}{\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow}\lim_{\mu\rightarrow\lambda}\iota_{\mu}^{<\lambda}\iota_{\mu}^{<\lambda}{}^{*}

is an isomorphism. This is proved by using induction on the poset, hence the conditions on
\Lambda
.

First let us do the base case(s). The trivial base case is when
\lambda
admits no smaller
\mu
(i.e. ‘minimal’), in which case
\cal C^{<\lambda}
is the trivial category, so that the claim is trivially true. The next base case is when
\lambda
admits ‘one layer’ of smaller
\mu
, namely there are minimal (and therefore mutually incomparable)
\mu_{i}<\lambda
. Then
\cal C^{<\lambda}=\prod_{i}\cal C^{\leq\mu_{i}}
, and as
\iota_{\mu}^{<\lambda}{}^{*}
can be easily seen to be the projection, again the claim is trivially true.

Now we do the inductive step. Let
\mu_{i}
be the maximal incomparable elements lower than
\lambda
, possible since
\Lambda
is locally-finite. We suppress the subscript when we talk about just one. Let
\cal C^{\ll\lambda}\coloneqq\langle\cal C^{<\mu_{i}}\rangle_{i}
be the category generated by the
\cal C^{<\mu_{i}}
. Consider the diagram:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cal C^{\ll\lambda}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cal C^{<\lambda}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cal C^{<\lambda}/\cal C^{\ll\lambda}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cal C^{<\mu}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cal C^{\leq\mu}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cal C^{=\mu}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{<\lambda}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\jmath^{<\lambda}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\mu}^{\ll\lambda}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\mu}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\jmath^{\mu}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\mu}^{<\lambda}}

Note that each row is a recollement. To save space we have not included the usual left adjoints. We claim there is an upward dotted map
\widetilde{\iota}_{\mu}^{<\lambda}\colon\cal C^{=\mu}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\cal C^{<\lambda}/\cal C^{\ll\lambda}
and a left adjoint
\widetilde{\iota}_{\mu}^{<\lambda}{}^{*}
such that

\widetilde{\iota}_{\mu}^{<\lambda}\jmath^{\mu}=\jmath^{<\lambda}\iota_{\mu}^{<\lambda},\hskip 20.00003pt\jmath^{\mu}_{!}\widetilde{\iota}_{\mu}^{<\lambda}{}^{*}=\iota_{\mu}^{<\lambda}{}^{*}\jmath^{<\lambda}_{!},

namely that the diagrams
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cal C^{<\lambda}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cal C^{<\lambda}/\cal C^{\ll\lambda}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cal C^{\leq\mu}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cal C^{=\mu}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\jmath^{<\lambda}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\jmath^{\mu}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\mu}^{<\lambda}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\iota}_{\mu}^{<\lambda}}

,
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cal C^{<\lambda}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cal C^{<\lambda}/\cal C^{\ll\lambda}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cal C^{\leq\mu}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cal C^{=\mu}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\mu}^{<\lambda}{}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\jmath^{<\lambda}_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\iota}_{\mu}^{<\lambda}{}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\jmath^{\mu}_{!}}

commute. Let us construct them as thus:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\iota}_{\mu}^{<\lambda}

\displaystyle=\jmath^{<\lambda}\iota_{\mu}^{<\lambda}\jmath^{\mu}_{*},

\displaystyle\widetilde{\iota}_{\mu}^{<\lambda}{}^{*}

\displaystyle=\jmath^{\mu}\iota_{\mu}^{<\lambda}{}^{*}\jmath^{<\lambda}_{!}.

Let us check that so-defined the diagrams commute. Indeed, one has from recollement and
\iota_{!}\iota^{!}\rightarrow\Id\rightarrow\jmath_{*}\jmath^{*}\rightarrow[+1]
that

\displaystyle\widetilde{\iota}_{\mu}^{<\lambda}\jmath^{\mu}=\jmath^{<\lambda}\iota_{\mu}^{<\lambda}\jmath^{\mu}_{*}\jmath^{\mu}=\operatorname{Cofib}(\underbrace{\jmath^{<\lambda}\iota_{\mu}^{<\lambda}\iota_{\mu}}_{=\jmath^{<\lambda}\iota_{<\lambda}\iota_{\mu}^{\ll\lambda}=0}\iota_{\mu}^{!}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\jmath^{<\lambda}\iota_{\mu}^{<\lambda})=\jmath^{<\lambda}\iota_{\mu}^{<\lambda},

which checks the first square, and similarly

\displaystyle\jmath^{\mu}_{!}\jmath^{\mu}\iota_{\mu}^{<\lambda}{}^{*}\jmath^{<\lambda}_{!}=\operatorname{Fib}(\iota_{\mu}^{<\lambda}{}^{*}\jmath^{<\lambda}_{!}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\iota_{\mu}\underbrace{\iota_{\mu}^{*}\iota_{\mu}^{<\lambda}{}^{*}\jmath^{<\lambda}_{!}}_{=\iota_{\mu}^{\ll\lambda}{}^{*}\iota_{<\lambda}^{*}\jmath^{<\lambda}_{!}=0})=\iota_{\mu}^{<\lambda}{}^{*}\jmath^{<\lambda}_{!}

checks the second square. So the maps we advertise do exist. Now, the different
\cal C^{=\mu_{i}}
form minimal mutually incomparable subcategories inside
\cal C^{<\lambda}/\cal C^{\ll\lambda}
, so by the base case of the induction we know

\Id_{\cal C^{<\lambda}/\cal C^{\ll\lambda}}\overset{\sim}{\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow}\lim_{\mu}\widetilde{\iota}_{\mu}^{<\lambda}\widetilde{\iota}_{\mu}^{<\lambda}{}^{*}.

But now note, since
\Id\overset{\sim}{\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow}\jmath^{\mu}\jmath^{\mu}_{!}
, we know

\displaystyle\lim_{\mu}\widetilde{\iota}_{\mu}^{<\lambda}\widetilde{\iota}_{\mu}^{<\lambda}{}^{*}=\lim_{\mu}\widetilde{\iota}_{\mu}^{<\lambda}\jmath^{\mu}\jmath^{\mu}_{!}\widetilde{\iota}_{\mu}^{<\lambda}{}^{*}=\lim_{\mu}\jmath^{<\lambda}\iota_{\mu}^{<\lambda}\iota_{\mu}^{<\lambda}{}^{*}\jmath^{<\lambda}_{!}=\jmath^{<\lambda}\lim_{\mu}(\iota_{\mu}^{<\lambda}\iota_{\mu}^{<\lambda}{}^{*})\jmath^{<\lambda}_{!},

so that combined with the above we know the map

\jmath^{<\lambda}_{!}\overset{\sim}{\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow}\lim_{\mu}\iota_{\mu}^{<\lambda}\iota_{\mu}^{<\lambda}{}^{*}\jmath^{<\lambda}_{!}

is an equivalence. Moreover, by induction, we know

\iota_{<\lambda}\overset{\sim}{\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow}\lim_{\mu}\iota_{\mu}^{<\lambda}\iota_{\mu}^{<\lambda}{}^{*}\iota_{<\lambda}

is also an equivalence. Together, this shows that

\Id_{\cal C^{<\lambda}}\overset{\sim}{\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow}\lim_{\mu}\iota_{\mu}^{<\lambda}\iota_{\mu}^{<\lambda}{}^{*}

is an equivalence due to the following general lemma.

The general lemma is that in a recollement situation
\cal C^{<}\rightarrow\cal C^{\leq}\rightarrow\cal C^{=}
, if
\cal F
is an endofunctor of
\cal C^{\leq}
which is the identity on the image of
\iota_{*}=\iota_{!}
and
\jmath_{!}
, then
\cal F
must be the identity. This is clearly true because the triangle
\jmath_{!}\jmath^{!}\rightarrow\Id\rightarrow\iota_{*}\iota^{*}
gives

\cal F\jmath_{!}\jmath^{!}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\cal F\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\cal F\iota_{*}\iota^{*}\overset{+1}{\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow},

and since
\cal F\jmath_{!}=\jmath_{!}
and
\cal F\iota_{*}=\iota_{*}
we conclude by the uniqueness of cones.

Now, if the poset
\Lambda
is down-finite, then this induction process will terminate. If it is instead locally finite and eventually disjoint-totally-ordered, then eventually the induction process will turn into proving this for a totally ordered set, where it is easily seen to be true, since if
\Lambda
is totally ordered and locally finite then for any
\lambda
there is a single maximal
\mu<\lambda
, whereupon
\cal C^{<\lambda}=\cal C^{\leq\mu}
.
∎

Remark
.

The above setup, with slight differences, has appeared for instance in
[
AMGR22
]
, Remarks 1.3.12 and 1.3.13 on pages 22–23. Our set-ups are slightly different (for example they require subcategories to have a tower of two right adjoints), but philosophically they should be roughly the same.

Remark
.

Also note that if
\Lambda
is down-finite (namely that for any
\lambda
the set
\{\mu:\mu\leq\lambda\}
is finite), then
\Lambda
automatically satisfies the conditions of the theorem. This is for instance the case with (a block of) category
\cal O
.

Let us say a few words about the maps in the spectral sequence in Theorem
above. The maps on the first page
E_{1}
are coming from the functoriality of the (co)cone; indeed, one has a
\mathbb{Z}
-filtration

\cdots\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\bigoplus_{\ell(\lambda)=i}\operatorname{\cal{I}\text{d}}(\lambda)\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\bigoplus_{\ell(\lambda)=i+1}\operatorname{\cal{I}\text{d}}(\lambda)\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\cdots,

where the morphisms are as constructed in the proof above, so that applying the functoriality of the fiber to

\bigoplus_{\ell(\lambda)=i}\operatorname{\cal{I}\text{d}}(\lambda)\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\bigoplus_{\ell(\lambda)=i+1}\operatorname{\cal{I}\text{d}}(\lambda)\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\bigoplus_{\ell(\lambda)=i+2}\operatorname{\cal{I}\text{d}}(\lambda)

we obtain the exact triangle

\bigoplus_{\ell(\lambda)=i}\operatorname{gr}^{\lambda}\operatorname{\cal{I}\text{d}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\operatorname{Fib}\left(\bigoplus_{\ell(\lambda)=i}\operatorname{\cal{I}\text{d}}(\lambda)\rightarrow\bigoplus_{\ell(\lambda)=i+2}\operatorname{\cal{I}\text{d}}(\lambda)\right)\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\bigoplus_{\ell(\lambda)=i+1}\operatorname{gr}^{\lambda}\operatorname{\cal{I}\text{d}}\overset{+1}{\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow};

rotating this gives a map

\bigoplus_{\ell(\lambda)=i+1}\operatorname{gr}^{\lambda}\operatorname{\cal{I}\text{d}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\bigoplus_{\ell(\lambda)=i}\operatorname{gr}^{\lambda}\operatorname{\cal{I}\text{d}}[1],

which under the action of taking
\pi
gives the differential maps of
E_{1}
.

Application to triangular-based algebras

Now let us return to the setting of the triangular based algebra
A
, which has lowest weight notation. Note that the recollements described in Section
2
give rise to a
\Theta^{\text{\,op}}
-filtration of the stable
\infty
-category
\mathsf{D}^{-}\operatorname{\mathsf{Mod}}A
where

(\mathsf{D}^{-}\operatorname{\mathsf{Mod}}A)^{\leq\theta}=\mathsf{D}^{-}\operatorname{\mathsf{Mod}}A^{\geq\theta}.

It is easy to see that

(\mathsf{D}^{-}\operatorname{\mathsf{Mod}}A)^{<\theta}=\mathsf{D}^{-}\operatorname{\mathsf{Mod}}A^{>\theta}=\mathsf{D}^{-}\operatorname{\mathsf{Mod}}(\raisebox{-1.49994pt}{\scalebox{1.32}[1.4]{$\nicefrac{{A^{\geq\theta}}}{{A^{\geq\theta}e^{\theta}A^{\geq\theta}}}$}}),

so that the Verdier quotient is
\mathsf{D}^{-}\operatorname{\mathsf{Mod}}A^{\theta}
. Then, by Theorem
, there exists a
\Theta
-filtration on the identity functor whose associated graded is

\displaystyle\operatorname{gr}^{\theta}\operatorname{\cal{I}\text{d}}=\overline{\iota}_{\theta}\jmath^{\theta}_{!}\jmath^{\theta}\overline{\iota}_{\theta}^{*}=\Delta(\theta)\otimes_{A^{\theta}}\operatorname{\mathsf{R}\text{Hom}}_{A}\left(\Delta(\theta),{\mathop{\square}}^{\dagger}\right)^{*},

where we recall the shorthand
\Delta(\theta)\coloneqq\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\theta}\Delta_{\lambda}^{\overline{l_{\lambda}(\theta)}}
. Moreover,
tells us that provided there is a length function
\ell\colon\Theta\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
, there is a spectral sequence (functorial in the input
{\mathop{\square}}
)

E_{1}^{p,q}=\bigoplus_{\ell(\theta)=-p}\Delta(\theta)\otimes_{A^{\theta}}\operatorname{Ext}^{-(p+q)}_{A}(\Delta(\theta),{\mathop{\square}}^{\dagger})^{*}\>\;\implies\>\;E_{\infty}^{p,q}=\operatorname{gr}^{-p}H^{p+q}({\mathop{\square}}).

To recover Theorem
for the nilBrauer algebra, consider the poset of weights
\Theta
for
{\text{N}\cal B}
as two disjoint totally ordered sets, namely
0<2<4<\cdots
and
1<3<5<\cdots
, equipped with a length function in which
\ell(2k)=\ell(2k+1)=k
. Then specializing to a block (either even or odd) recovers
.

Remark
.

One could just as well consider
\Theta
for nilBrauer to be totally ordered as
0<1<2<\cdots
; in this case the spectral sequence would look different but be equivalent.

To describe this spectral sequence in more detail for the nilBrauer setting, it is then crucial to investigate the Ext groups appearing on the first page.

4.
Standard Ext Groups, Part 1: Nilcohomology

In order to obtain a BGG resolution of
L
using this philosophy of reconstructing the identity functor, one needs to compute these Ext groups,
\operatorname{Ext}_{A}^{\bullet}(\Delta(\theta),L)
. Either way, the Ext groups between standard modules and simples are interesting alone, being the analogue of the Kazhdan-Lusztig problem. As they are Ext groups out of standard modules rather than proper standard modules, we shall call them “standard Ext groups”, or “big Ext groups” (after ‘big Verma’). We attack this by emulating Lie algebra nilcohomology.

Recall that
\operatorname{Ext}^{\bullet}(\Delta_{\lambda},{\mathop{\square}})=H^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{n}^{+}:{\mathop{\square}})^{\lambda}
in category
\cal O
, where the key was to write this as
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}\text{Hom}}_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}(\mathbb{C}_{0},{\mathop{\square}})
, which admits an action of
\mathfrak{h}
. This in turn can be computed with the Koszul resolution of
\mathbb{C}_{0}
, which importantly is a finite resolution (because it is built from wedge powers of
\mathfrak{n}^{+}
, which is finite-dimensional). This is the story we will try to emulate.

The nilalgebra and nilcohomology

In the below story, we have written
A
instead of
{\text{N}\cal B}
to indicate that this procedure is general. Let
\mathbb{K}=\bigoplus\mathbbm{k}e^{\theta}
, and let the “nilalgebra” be defined as

A^{-}\coloneqq\mathbb{K}\text{Y}=\mathbb{K}\oplus\mathop{\bigoplus\bigoplus}_{\psi\not\geq\theta}e^{\psi}\mathbb{C}\text{Y}_{+}e^{\theta},

where the subscript in
\text{Y}_{+}
indicates that the diagram
e^{\theta}
is not included. Note that in the nilBrauer case one may as well take the bottom index to run over
\psi<\theta
of the same parity. This is the analogue of
U\mathfrak{n}^{+}
. Of course one must check on a case-by-case basis that, so defined, this
A^{-}
is actually a subalgebra of
A
, i.e. that it is closed under multiplication. This is typically not difficult, and we check it now for the nilBrauer:

\vbox{}\left.\vbox{\vbox to1.6003pt{\vfil}}\right[\vbox{\vskip-7.5pt\begin{lemma}So defined, ${\text{N}\cal B}^{-}$ is a subalgebra of ${\text{N}\cal B}$.\end{lemma}\vskip-6.0pt}

Proof.

It is important to note here that, due to the way the PBW basis theorem is stated for nilBrauer, the diagrams of
\mathbb{C}\text{Y}_{+}
have dots in ‘preferred’ locations: it is our choice to have dots on the far left rather than the right of each cap. One needs to check that
{\text{N}\cal B}^{-}
, so defined, is closed under multiplication: namely that when two diagrams, each with dots only at the left base of each cap, are stacked with each other, one obtains a linear combination of diagrams also with dots only at the left base of each cap. This is true because the dots in the top diagram must connect with propagating strings of the bottom diagram, but propagating strings of the bottom diagram can only cross with caps by assumption (and not other propagating strings). Then the relation to observe is simply (here the orange lines are not part of the diagrammatics and are visual aids placed to make clear where the diagram starts/ends)
