Hence, when multiplying a diagram with another diagram, the dots on the top diagram can be moved to be at the left base of the resulting product diagram, at the cost of error terms which (e.g. by induction) are also in
\mathbb{C}\text{Y}
. This shows
\mathbb{C}\text{Y}
is closed under multiplication, as desired.∎

To return to the general picture, this
A^{-}
then has an ideal

I\coloneqq A^{-}_{+}\coloneqq\mathop{\bigoplus\bigoplus}_{\psi\not\geq\theta}e^{\psi}\mathbb{C}\text{Y}_{+}e^{\theta},

which is analogous to the positive degree elements of
U\mathfrak{n}^{+}
. Once it is shown that
A^{-}
is a subalgebra of
A
, it is evidently automatic that
I
is an ideal. Note that
A^{-}/I=\mathbb{K}
, and that there is an action

A^{-}\mathrel{\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin]{270.0}{$\circlearrowright$}}}\mathbbm{k}e^{\theta}

where all of
I
acts by zero and
\mathbb{K}
acts in the obvious way by multiplication. We can consider the obvious modification of the bar resolution in order to resolve
\mathbbm{k}e^{\theta}
with free
A^{-}
-modules (here all tensors are over
\mathbb{K}
):

\cdots\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow A^{-}\otimes I^{\otimes k}e^{\theta}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\cdots\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow A^{-}\otimes I\otimes Ie^{\theta}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow A^{-}\otimes Ie^{\theta}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow A^{-}e^{\theta}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathbbm{k}e^{\theta},

where the maps are (for brevity we write
(a_{1},\cdots\hskip-0.1pt,a_{k})
for
a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{k}
)

\displaystyle\partial_{k}\colon A^{-}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}I^{\otimes k}e^{\theta}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow A^{-}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}I^{\otimes k-1}e^{\theta}

\displaystyle x\otimes(a_{1},\cdots\hskip-0.1pt,a_{k}e^{\theta})

\displaystyle\longmapsto xa_{1}\otimes(a_{2},\cdots\hskip-0.1pt,a_{k}e^{\theta})+\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}(-1)^{i}x\otimes(a_{1},\cdots\hskip-0.1pt,a_{i}a_{i+1},\cdots\hskip-0.1pt,a_{k}e^{\theta}).

This can be shown to be exact by using the obvious modification of the usual homotopy: send
x\otimes a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{k}
to
1\otimes\pi_{+}(x)\otimes a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{k}
, where
\pi_{+}\colon A^{-}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow I
is projection away from
A^{-}_{0}=\mathbb{K}
.

Now, note

\Hom_{A}(A^{\geq\theta}e^{\theta},M)\cong\Hom_{A^{-}}(\mathbbm{k}e^{\theta},M)

This is because a map on the LHS is determined by where it sends
e^{\theta}
, and since the map must commute with all of
A
, it must send
e^{\theta}
to something that is killed by anything passing through
e^{\psi}
for
\psi\not\geq\theta
. A similar analysis of the RHS shows these two are isomorphic, and both are isomorphic to
(M^{A^{-}})^{\theta}
, where

M^{A^{-}}\coloneqq\{v\in M:I\cdot v=0\}

is the submodule of vectors killed by
I
. This is all in analogy
4
4

4

The fact that the plus sign and minus sign are switched is a reflection of the fact that the nilBrauer representation theory is lowest weight rather than highest weight.
to the observation from Lie theory of
\Hom O(\Delta_{\lambda},M)=\Hom_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}(\mathbb{C}_{\lambda},M)=M^{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}
. Note well that the left
A^{\theta}
-action on the LHS of Equation (
4
), which came from the right
A^{\theta}
-action on
A^{\geq\theta}e^{\theta}
, becomes the left action on the RHS inherited from
M
. The issue with this is that the
A^{\theta}
-action on the RHS becomes ‘lost’ when we take derived functors, in the sense that the above complex,
A^{-}\otimes I^{\otimes\bullet}e^{\theta}
, does not have a right
A^{\theta}
-action. Secretly it must still be there, but one cannot compute it using this complex. Hence we may define (the
\theta
-weight space of) the “nilcohomology” as

H^{\bullet}(A^{-}:M)^{\theta}\coloneqq\operatorname{\mathsf{R}\text{Hom}}_{A}(A^{\geq\theta}e^{\theta},M)\overset{\mathsf{Vec}}{\cong}\operatorname{\mathsf{R}\text{Hom}}_{A^{-}}(\mathbbm{k}e^{\theta},M)

which can be computed as the cohomology of the complex

\hskip-180.00027pt0\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\Hom_{A^{-}}(A^{-}e^{\theta},M)\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\Hom_{A^{-}}(A^{-}\otimes Ie^{\theta},M)\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\cdots

\iddots

\hskip 150.00023pt\cdots\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\Hom_{A^{-}}(A^{-}\otimes I^{\otimes k}e^{\theta},M)\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\Hom_{A^{-}}(A^{-}\otimes I^{\otimes k+1}e^{\theta},M)\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\cdots

This complex can be rewritten using tensor-Hom as

\hskip-200.0003pt0\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\Hom_{\mathbb{K}}(\mathbbm{k}e^{\theta},M)\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\Hom_{\mathbb{K}}(Ie^{\theta},M)\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\cdots

\hskip-30.00005pt\iddots

\hskip 180.00027pt\cdots\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\Hom_{\mathbb{K}}(I^{\otimes k}e^{\theta},M)\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\Hom_{\mathbb{K}}(I^{\otimes k+1}e^{\theta},M)\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\cdots

where each differential
\textnormal{d}^{k}(f)=f(\partial_{k+1}{\mathop{\square}})
can be simply found to be

\displaystyle\textnormal{d}^{k}\colon\Hom_{\mathbb{K}}(I^{\otimes k}e^{\theta},M)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\Hom_{\mathbb{K}}(I^{\otimes k+1}e^{\theta},M)

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle\longmapsto\bigg((a_{1},\cdots\hskip-0.1pt,a_{k+1}e^{\theta})\mapstochar\rightarrow a_{1}f(a_{2},\cdots\hskip-0.1pt,a_{k+1}e^{\theta})+\sum_{i=1}^{k}(-1)^{i}f(a_{1},\cdots\hskip-0.1pt,a_{i}a_{i+1},\cdots\hskip-0.1pt,a_{k+1}e^{\theta})\bigg).

This story can be made to even more closely resemble the Lie algebra one by taking a more ‘uniform’ approach – consider the action
A^{-}\mathrel{\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin]{270.0}{$\circlearrowright$}}}\mathbb{K}
instead of the action on
\mathbbm{k}e^{\theta}
; then
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}\text{Hom}}_{A^{-}}(\mathbb{K},M)
has a left action of
\mathbb{K}
, and we can take the weight spaces with respect to this action to get each

H^{\bullet}(A^{-}:M)^{\theta}=e^{\theta}\operatorname{Ext}_{A^{-}}^{\bullet}(\mathbb{K},M)=\operatorname{Ext}_{A}^{\bullet}(A^{\geq\theta}e^{\theta},M)=\operatorname{Ext}_{A}^{\bullet}(\Delta(\theta),M).

This
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}\text{Hom}}_{A^{-}}(\mathbb{K},M)
then deserves the name “nilcohomology”; we will call this

H^{\bullet}(A^{-}:M)\coloneqq\operatorname{Ext}_{A^{-}}^{\bullet}(\mathbb{K},M).

This is computed with the complex

\cdots\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow A^{-}\otimes I^{\otimes k}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\cdots\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow A^{-}\otimes I\otimes I\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow A^{-}\otimes I\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow A^{-}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathbb{K},

which ‘weight-specializes’ to the complex
A^{-}\otimes I^{\otimes\bullet}e^{\theta}
considered above. More explicitly,

H^{\bullet}(A^{-}:M)\simeq\Hom_{\mathbb{K}}(I^{\otimes\bullet},M),

where the differentials are identical to
\textnormal{d}^{k}
computed above except erasing each appearance of
e^{\theta}
. Worth saying is that, also in analogy with Lie theory,
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}\text{Hom}}_{A^{-}}^{\bullet}(\mathbb{K},M)
is the right derived functor of the invariant functor
M\mapstochar\rightarrow M^{A^{-}}
which picks out the set of vectors killed by
I
. So again one has a cohomology theory obtained from requiring a ‘nilalgebra’ to act by zero.

Remark
.

A warning though: this incarnation of the nilcohomology, using the bar complex above, loses the left
A^{\theta}
-action on
H^{\bullet}(A^{-}:M)^{\theta}
due to the lack of an obvious right
A^{\theta}
-action on
A^{-}\otimes I^{\otimes k}
. We will fix this later in this Section.

From this resolution one can immediately say something weak about where the standard Ext groups vanish. As the tensors are over
\mathbb{K}
, they must be permeable to the idempotents
e^{\theta}
, so that a tensor
a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{k}\in I\otimes\cdots\otimes I
must have the beginning and the ending idempotents of each consecutive
a_{i}
match up. In other words, it must be that
a_{i}=a_{i}e^{\theta_{i}}
and
a_{i+1}=e^{\theta_{i}}a_{i+1}
for each
i
. In the nilBrauer case, this means for instance that

\text{$I^{\otimes k}e^{\theta}=0$ if $k>\theta/2$},

since elements of
I
decrease the weight by at least 2. In this respect this story resembles that of Lie algebra nilcohomology even further. This means in particular we have the (very weak) bound

\vbox{}\left.\vbox{\vbox to28.59033pt{\vfil}}\right[\vbox{\vskip-7.5pt\begin{lemma}$$\operatorname{Ext}_{\text{N}\cal B}^{>\theta/2}({\text{N}\cal B}^{\geq\theta}e^{\theta},M)=0,$$so in particular
$$\operatorname{Ext}_{\text{N}\cal B}^{>\theta/2}(\Delta_{\theta},M)=0.$$\end{lemma}\vskip-6.0pt}

The above is true for all
M
, and the results can be improved depending on the lowest weight of
M
, since the Homs must moreover respect the
\mathbb{K}
-action on the first tensor factor (i.e. maps must send
a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{k}
to the weight space
M^{\psi}
if
a_{1}=e^{\psi}a_{1}
):

\vbox{}\left.\vbox{\vbox to50.71141pt{\vfil}}\right[\vbox{\vskip-7.5pt\begin{lemma}If $M$ has lowest weight $\psi$ of the same parity as $\theta$ (all such $\operatorname{\mathsf{R}\text{Hom}}$'s vanish if they are not of the same parity), then
$$\operatorname{Ext}_{\text{N}\cal B}^{>\frac{\theta-\psi}{2}}({\text{N}\cal B}^{\geq\theta}e^{\theta},M)=0,$$and in particular
$$\operatorname{Ext}_{\text{N}\cal B}^{>\frac{\theta-\psi}{2}}(\Delta_{\theta},M)=0.$$\end{lemma}\vskip-6.0pt}

These cohomology vanishing results indicate that the main obstruction to
\operatorname{\mathsf{Mod}}{\text{N}\cal B}
having finite global dimension is the fact that there are infinitely many standard objects (corresponding to infinitely many idempotents). If one were to ‘truncate’ the algebra
{\text{N}\cal B}
in a stupid manner, for example simply by deleting all diagrams that pass through more than
\theta
strands, then one would obtain an algebra whose module category had finite global dimension.

It is perhaps worth pointing out, as a special case of the constructions above, that for the module
A^{-}\mathrel{\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin]{270.0}{$\circlearrowright$}}}\mathbb{K}
, the nilcohomology
H^{\bullet}(A^{-}:\mathbb{K})=\operatorname{\mathsf{R}\text{Hom}}_{A^{-}}^{\bullet}(\mathbb{K},\mathbb{K})
can be computed as

\displaystyle\operatorname{Ext}^{\bullet}_{A^{-}}(\mathbb{K},\mathbb{K})

\displaystyle=H^{\bullet}\big(\Hom_{A^{-}}(A^{-}\otimes I^{\otimes\bullet},\mathbb{K})\big)

\displaystyle=H^{\bullet}\big(\Hom_{\mathbb{K}}(I^{\otimes\bullet},\mathbb{K})\big)

\displaystyle=H^{-\bullet}(I^{\otimes\bullet}).

Also worth noting is that, in the case that
A^{-}
is Koszul, we know that

\operatorname{Ext}^{\bullet}_{A^{-}}(\mathbb{K},\mathbb{K})\cong A^{-,!,\text{\,op}}.

Using the right actions of
\mathbb{K}\mathrel{\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\circlearrowleft$}}}\mathbb{K}
for both entries to obtain left and right
\mathbb{K}
-actions on the Ext group, we then have

e^{\phi}A^{-,!}e^{\theta}=e^{\theta}\operatorname{Ext}^{\bullet}_{A^{-}}(\mathbb{K},\mathbb{K})e^{\phi}=\operatorname{Ext}^{\bullet}_{A^{-}}(\mathbbm{k}e^{\theta},\mathbbm{k}e^{\phi}).

Then the nilcohomology assumes his multi-armed form

We had remarked earlier that our earlier construction of nilcohomology using
A^{-}
fails to reproduce the
A^{\theta}
-action. In this section we remedy this. This is accomplished by defining another subalgebra
A^{\flat}
, which is roughly a ‘bigger’ version of
A^{-}
; computing nilcohomology with this
A^{\flat}
will reproduce the
A^{\theta}
-action. One might wonder why the earlier construction was necessary at all then – the reason is because the former is easier to handle, and indeed one at least has a chance of proving that
A^{-}
is Koszul, as it at least has a semisimple degree 0 part. The algebra we will now define does not admit Koszul methods as simply (as far as we can tell).

Define the locally unital subalgebra of
A

A^{\flat}\coloneqq\bigoplus_{\theta\leq\phi}e^{\theta}Ae^{\phi}.

From this definition, it is clear that this is a subalgebra of
A
. Note that cup-caps are allowed as elements of this subalgebra in the case of
A={\text{N}\cal B}
. Also define the locally unital subalgebra

A^{\natural}\coloneqq\bigoplus_{\theta}e^{\theta}Ae^{\theta},

which is a quotient of
A^{\flat}
by the ideal

A^{\flat}_{+}\coloneqq\bigoplus_{\theta<\phi}e^{\theta}Ae^{\phi}.

Contrast
A^{\natural}
with

A^{\circ}\coloneqq\bigoplus_{\theta}A^{\theta},

which is not a subalgebra, but rather a subquotient. There is a quotient map

0\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathfrak{k}=e^{\theta}\langle e^{\phi}:\phi\not\geq\theta\rangle e^{\theta}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow A^{\natural}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow A^{\circ}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow 0,

and also

0\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow I^{\flat}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow A^{\flat}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow A^{\circ}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow 0,

where

I^{\flat}\coloneqq\langle\mathfrak{k},A^{\flat}_{+}\rangle.

In the case of nilBrauer, we can put an explicit grading on
{\text{N}\cal B}^{\flat}
. Let

{\text{N}\cal B}^{\flat}_{i}\coloneqq\bigoplus_{\theta}e^{\theta-2i}{\text{N}\cal B}e^{\theta},

which is to say the
i
-th graded part are the diagrams with
i
more caps than cups. Under this grading
{\text{N}\cal B}^{\flat}_{0}={\text{N}\cal B}^{\natural}
, and

{\text{N}\cal B}^{\flat}_{+}=\bigoplus_{i\geq 1}{\text{N}\cal B}^{\flat}_{i}.

Then one makes a similar observation to before, that

\Hom_{A}(A^{\geq\theta}e^{\theta},M)=\Hom_{A^{-}}(\mathbbm{k}e^{\theta},M)=\Hom_{A^{\flat}}(A^{\theta},M).

The ‘uniform’ (with respect to weights) statement of this would be

\Hom_{A}\left(\bigoplus_{\theta}A^{\geq\theta}e^{\theta},M\right)=\Hom_{A^{-}}(\mathbb{K},M)=\Hom_{A^{\flat}}(A^{\circ},M).

The benefit of this is that now
\Hom_{A^{\flat}}(A^{\theta},M)
admits a left
A^{\theta}
-action coming from the right action on the first input. Note that we can also think of this action as an
A^{\natural}
-action coming from
A^{\circ}\mathrel{\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\circlearrowleft$}}}A^{\natural}
, which moreover factors to an
A^{\circ}
-action. To compute the derived functors, we utilize also a bar resolution. This time consider the resolution

\cdots\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow A^{\flat}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}I^{\flat,\otimes_{A^{\natural}}k}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\cdots\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow A^{\flat}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}I^{\flat}\otimes_{A^{\natural}}I^{\flat}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow A^{\flat}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}I^{\flat}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow A^{\flat}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow A^{\circ},

which would weight-specialize to

\cdots\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow A^{\flat}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}I^{\flat,\otimes_{A^{\natural}}k}e^{\theta}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\cdots\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow A^{\flat}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}I^{\flat}\otimes_{A^{\natural}}I^{\flat}e^{\theta}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow A^{\flat}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}I^{\flat}e^{\theta}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow A^{\flat}e^{\theta}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow A^{\theta}.

This resolution is by
(A^{\flat},A^{\natural})
-bimodules which are free on the left (due to the left-most tensor being over
\mathbb{K}
), and moreover there is a right
A^{\natural}
-action coming from
I^{\flat}\mathrel{\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\circlearrowleft$}}}A^{\natural}
. Note well that the first tensor in each term is over
\mathbb{K}
, while subsequent tensors are over
A^{\natural}
. Then each term of the complex which computes Ext

\Hom_{A^{\flat}}(A^{\flat},M)\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\Hom_{A^{\flat}}(A^{\flat}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}I^{\flat},M)\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\Hom_{A^{\flat}}(A^{\flat}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}I^{\flat}\otimes_{A^{\natural}}I^{\flat},M)\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\cdots

admits a left
A^{\natural}
-action, and because this
A^{\natural}
-action is respected by the complex and because it coincides with the
A^{\natural}
-action on
\Hom_{A^{\flat}}(A^{\circ},M)
which factors through
A^{\circ}
, when one takes (co)homology of this complex to get the Ext groups, this
A^{\natural}
-action on the Ext groups must also factor through to a
A^{\circ}
-action. Specializing to a weight, we get an incarnation of nilcohomology which describes the action of the Cartans
A^{\theta}
. When we wish to emphasize that this
A^{\flat}
-resolution of
A^{\circ}
is what we are using, we may add a subscript
H^{\bullet}_{\flat}(A^{-}:M)
to nilcohomology.

5.
Standard Ext Groups, Part 2: Koszulness

Recall that a Kostant module is one for which the nilcohomology/standard Ext groups are appropriately concentrated; for example, the finite-dimensional simple modules of category
\cal O
are Kostant modules. We wish to show
L_{0}=\mathbbm{k}e^{0}
(at
t=0
) is a Kostant module. It turns out this will be the same as showing the algebra
A^{-}={\text{N}\cal B}^{-}
is Koszul. This section is devoted to the proof that
{\text{N}\cal B}^{-}
is Koszul. But first we must do more set up; for instance, what is even the grading with respect to which Koszulness is proved?

The Koszul Grading

Recall
{\text{N}\cal B}^{-}=\mathbb{K}\oplus\displaystyle\mathop{\bigoplus\bigoplus}_{\psi<\theta}e^{\psi}\mathbb{C}\text{Y}_{+}e^{\theta}
. Endow an additional grading on this algebra, appropriately called the “cap grading”:

\deg_{\text{cap}}(\text{diagram})=\#\text{ of caps}.

We use the name ‘Koszul grading’ interchangeably. Let

{\text{N}\cal B}^{-}_{i}\coloneqq\text{$\mathbb{C}$-span of all cap-degree-$i$ $\text{Y}$-diagrams},

so that

{\text{N}\cal B}^{-}=\bigoplus_{i=0}^{\infty}{\text{N}\cal B}^{-}_{i}.

Note that we have put the cap grading in the subscript; the cap grading induces a grading on Ext groups, which we shall later also denote in the subscript.

Remark
.

Warning: there are now two gradings at play, the cap grading and the quantum grading (or ‘
q
-grading’). The former counts the number of caps, while the latter counts crossings and dots. There is also another sort of ‘grading’ coming from the weights/idempotents
e^{\theta}
; note that once you know the number of caps and the weight you start at, the weight you end at is determined, so perhaps this is only half a grading. Later there will also be a homological grading. So this story is sort of triply-and-a-half graded. We will also later consider a ‘dot filtration’, counting the total number of dots.

Maybe now is a good time to remind ourselves that the most important relation in
{\text{N}\cal B}
is

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 10.86978pt\hbox{{\hbox{\kern-10.86978pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-10.86978pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\hbox to21.74pt{\vbox to21.74pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

If one of the strands is a propagating string, we keep the dot on the left. It is clear this is not affected by composition of diagrams.

The first important observation is that so defined, this graded algebra is quadratic. Indeed, every diagram that has multiple caps can be written as the vertical composition of multiple diagrams each with a single cap. So there is a surjective map

{\bigotimes}^{\bullet}{\text{N}\cal B}^{-}_{1}\relbar\joinrel\twoheadrightarrow{\text{N}\cal B}^{-}

given by multiplication. More importantly, the kernel
\mathfrak{q}
of this map

0\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathfrak{q}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow{\bigotimes}^{\bullet}{\text{N}\cal B}^{-}_{1}\relbar\joinrel\twoheadrightarrow{\text{N}\cal B}^{-}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow 0

is a ‘quadratic ideal’, meaning that it is generated by a subspace of
{\text{N}\cal B}^{-}_{1}\otimes{\text{N}\cal B}^{-}_{1}
. For example, up to dots, the relations inside
{\text{N}\cal B}^{-}
look like

\displaystyle=\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 21.73956pt\hbox{{\hbox{\kern-21.73956pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-21.73956pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\hbox to43.48pt{\vbox to43.48pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

is already true in

.

That the ideal
\mathfrak{q}
is quadratic is intuitively obvious and an artifact of the setting of diagrammatic algebras; the specific relations of nilBrauer are not important for this to be true.

\vbox{}\left.\vbox{\vbox to1.6003pt{\vfil}}\right[\vbox{\vskip-7.5pt\begin{lemma}The ideal $\mathfrak{q}$ is quadratic.\end{lemma}\vskip-6.0pt}

Proof.

One way to see this is to appeal to
[
PP05
, Chapter 1, Corollary 1.5.3]
:

\vbox{}\left.\vbox{\vbox to15.83336pt{\vfil}}\right[\vbox{\vskip-7.5pt\begin{lemma}$A$ is quadratic iff $\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{A}{}_{j}(\mathbb{K},\mathbb{K})=0$ for $j>i$ and $i=1,2$; $A$ is one-generated iff $\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{A}{}_{j}(\mathbb{K},\mathbb{K})=0$ for $j>i$ and $i=1$.\end{lemma}\vskip-6.0pt}

We have already seen that
{\text{N}\cal B}^{-}
is one-generated, so it suffices to consider
i=2
. Using the resolutions constructed in Section
4
, one can compute
\operatorname{Ext}_{{\text{N}\cal B}^{-}}^{2}(\mathbb{K},\mathbb{K})
as
\Ker/\img
, where
\Ker
and
\img
are the subspaces of
\Hom_{\mathbb{K}}(I\otimes I,\mathbb{K})
which are

\Ker=\{f:f(ab\otimes c)=f(a\otimes bc)\},\hskip 10.00002pt\img=\{f:f(a\otimes b)=-g(ab)\text{ for }g\in I^{*}\}.

The claim is that any
f\in\Ker/\img
must vanish on tensors
x\otimes y
which have
\deg_{\text{cap}}xy\geq 3
, i.e. that any
f\in\Ker
must be constant on inputs which multiply (
\mu\colon x\otimes y\mapstochar\rightarrow xy
) to the same algebra element of cap-degree
\geq 3
. This is true for essentially the same reason the Eckmann-Hilton argument works, which is also why this only works for cap-degree
\geq 3
.

One can for example observe that there is a basis of
{\text{N}\cal B}^{-}
in which the caps are visually arranged so that, for example, the highest cap is at the far left and the lowest cap is at the far right. This arrangement is mostly cosmetic; the difference is that dots live on the top-left of caps, so members of this basis have dots which are arranged diagonally from the top-left to the bottom-right. Then, given any
\sum x_{i}\otimes y_{i}
multiplying to a fixed element
z
of degree
\geq 3
, we can rewrite it according to this basis and keep pushing caps across the tensor sign until we arrive at something of form
\sum a_{i}\otimes z_{i}
, where
\deg_{\text{cap}}a_{i}=1
and
a_{i}z_{i}
are diagrams in this basis. This process does not change the value of
f\in\Ker
, and the final result is independent of the form of
\sum x_{i}\otimes y_{i}
, as long as it multiplies to
z
.∎

One can now form the quadratic dual of
{\text{N}\cal B}^{-}
, which would also be called the Koszul dual if
{\text{N}\cal B}^{-}
were Koszul.

The Koszul claim

At this point, the claim is then that
{\text{N}\cal B}^{-}
is not only quadratic but moreover Koszul. Firstly, let us recall (here
A^{!}
refers to the quadratic dual of an algebra)

\vbox{}\left.\vbox{\vbox to104.4167pt{\vfil}}\right(\vbox{\vskip-7.5pt\begin{definition}A graded (locally unital) algebra $A$ with $A_{0}=\mathbb{K}$ is called ``Koszul'' if the following equivalent conditions hold:
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@$\operatorname{Ext}_{A}^{i}{}_{j}=0$ for $i\neq j$;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@$A$ is one-generated and the algebra $\operatorname{Ext}_{A}^{\bullet}(\mathbb{K},\mathbb{K})$ is generated by $\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{A}(\mathbb{K},\mathbb{K})$;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@$A$ is quadratic and $\operatorname{Ext}_{A}^{\bullet}(\mathbb{K},\mathbb{K})\cong A^{!}$;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@the algebra $\operatorname{Ext}_{A}^{\bullet}(\mathbb{K},\mathbb{K})$, equipped with the cap grading, is one-generated.
\end{itemize}\end{definition}\vskip-7.5pt}

We then claim that

\vbox{}\left.\vbox{\vbox to1.6003pt{\vfil}}\right[\vbox{\vskip-7.5pt\begin{theorem}The quadratic algebra ${\text{N}\cal B}^{-}$ is Koszul.\end{theorem}\vskip-6.0pt}

Remark
.

We should remark that in the argument spanning the following pages, we make use of two important facts, 4.7.1 and 2.4.1, from
[
PP05
]
; a review of the proofs of these facts there reveals that the same proofs go through for locally graded-finite-dimensional algebras.

Let us briefly sketch the plan for the proof. The overall plan is to prove
{\text{N}\cal B}^{-}
is Koszul by putting a filtration (the ‘dot filtration’) on it and showing the associated graded is Koszul; by general Koszul theory this then shows the original
{\text{N}\cal B}^{-}
is Koszul. To show the associated graded is Koszul we will use the characterization of Koszulness using distributive lattices. To do this we will find a distributing basis by dividing-and-conquering according to so-called ‘intersection/nesting patterns’. And lastly for each fixed intersection/nesting pattern, the problem is reduced to a more classical problem which can be solved using general Koszul theory.

Filtrations on quadratic algebras

First we need to define what it means to be a filtered quadratic algebra.

\vbox{}\left.\vbox{\vbox to61.21141pt{\vfil}}\right(\vbox{\vskip-7.5pt\begin{definition}A ``graded ordered semigroup'' $\Gamma$ is a (not necessarily commutative) semigroup with unit $e$ equipped with a homomorphism
$$g\colon\Gamma\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}$$and total orders on the fibers $g^{-1}(n)=\Gamma_{n}$ such that
$$\alpha<\beta\implies\alpha\gamma<\beta\gamma,\ \gamma\alpha<\gamma\beta\>\;\forall\;\alpha,\beta\in\Gamma_{n},\ \gamma\in\Gamma_{k}$$and $g^{-1}(0)=\{e\}$.\end{definition}\vskip-7.5pt}

Here the symbol
\Gamma
is not to be confused with the ring of Schur
q
-functions from earlier. We remark that for us it is best to think of the semigroup
\Gamma
additively; for instance we will write
e
as 0 and use
+
.

\vbox{}\left.\vbox{\vbox to158.43779pt{\vfil}}\right(\vbox{\vskip-7.5pt\begin{definition}A ``$\Gamma$-valued filtration'' on a graded algebra $A$ is a collection of subspaces $\text{F}_{\alpha}A_{n}\subseteq A_{n}$ for every $\alpha\in\Gamma_{n}$ and $n\geq 0$ such that
\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@$\alpha\leq\beta\implies\text{F}_{\alpha}A_{n}\subseteq\text{F}_{\beta}A_{n}$,
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@$\text{F}_{\infty_{n}}A_{n}=A_{n}$ for $\infty_{n}$ the maximal element of $\Gamma_{n}$, and
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@$\text{F}_{\alpha}A_{n}\cdot\text{F}_{\beta}A_{m}\subseteq\text{F}_{\alpha+\beta}A_{n+m}$.
\end{enumerate}Then the ``associated graded'' of $A$ is
$$\operatorname{gr}^{\text{F}}A=\bigoplus_{\alpha}\text{F}_{\alpha}A_{n}/\text{F}_{\alpha^{\prime}}A_{n},$$where $\alpha^{\prime}\lessdot\alpha$.
\par This filtration is said to be `one-generated' if $\text{F}_{\alpha}A_{n}=\sum_{i_{1}+\cdots+i_{n}\leq\alpha}\text{F}_{i_{1}}A_{1}\cdots\text{F}_{i_{n}}A_{1}$.\end{definition}\vskip-7.5pt}

Note that
F
is one-generated if and only if
\operatorname{gr}^{\text{F}}A
is one-generated.

Then a fact from general Koszul theory is

\vbox{}\left.\vbox{\vbox to13.6003pt{\vfil}}\right[\vbox{\vskip-7.5pt\begin{theorem}If $A$ is a quadratic algebra equipped with a one-generated $\Gamma$-valued filtration, then Koszulness of $\operatorname{gr}^{\text{F}}A$ implies Koszulness of $A$.\end{theorem}\vskip-6.0pt}

We will thus give a filtration, called the “dot filtration”, on
{\text{N}\cal B}^{-}
, and prove that its associated graded is Koszul. The benefit of this filtration is that the dot-sliding rules are vastly simplified in the associated graded.

Let
\overline{\mathbb{N}}=\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
. Consider the (additive) semigroup

\Gamma=(0,0)\sqcup\bigsqcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}i>0\\
j\in\overline{\mathbb{N}}\end{subarray}}(i,j),

where
(0,0)
is the unit and the operation is
(i,j)+(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})=(i+i^{\prime},j+j^{\prime})
. This comes with a homomorphism

\displaystyle g\colon\Gamma

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle(i,j)

\displaystyle\longmapsto i,

with each fiber
g^{-1}(i)\cong\overline{\mathbb{N}}
(
i\neq 0
) coming with the usual total order
0\leq 1\leq 2\leq\cdots\leq\infty
. Then we endow
{\text{N}\cal B}^{-}
with a
\Gamma
-valued filtration
\text{F}=\text{F}^{\text{dot}}
by letting

\text{F}_{j}{\text{N}\cal B}_{i}^{-}\coloneqq\text{F}_{(i,j)}{\text{N}\cal B}_{i}^{-}=\text{diagrams with $\leq j$ total dots on $i$ caps}.

Of course, if there are no caps at all, then there is nowhere to put dots. This filtration is moreover one-generated since given any diagram with
i
caps and
j
dots (WLOG dots placed on caps), one can write it as the product of
i
diagrams, each with a single cap and some number of dots on it, such that the total number of dots is
j
. So for instance

\text{F}_{0}{\text{N}\cal B}_{i}^{-}\subset\text{F}_{1}{\text{N}\cal B}_{i}^{-}\subset\text{F}_{2}{\text{N}\cal B}_{i}^{-}\subset\cdots\subset\text{F}_{\infty}{\text{N}\cal B}_{i}^{-}={\text{N}\cal B}_{i}^{-}.

The associated graded,
\operatorname{gr}^{\text{F}}{\text{N}\cal B}^{-}
, is much simpler. Indeed, modulo diagrams with at most a strictly smaller number of dots, dots slide perfectly across crossings; for example:

\displaystyle\in\mathfrak{p}\coloneqq\text{the quadratic ideal of }\operatorname{gr}^{\text{F}}{\text{N}\cal B}^{-}.

We will now venture forth to show that
\operatorname{gr}^{\text{F}}{\text{N}\cal B}^{-}
is Koszul. For shorthand let us write

B\coloneqq\operatorname{gr}^{\text{F}}{\text{N}\cal B}^{-},

with quadratic kernel
\mathfrak{p}
(not a prime ideal; this is not a notational conflict because we will not be considering prime ideals).

Remark
.

This filtration
F
can likely be further refined to control the number of dots on each string, e.g. at most
j_{1}
dots on string number 1, etc.; however we do not take this approach here.

Distributive lattices and nesting patterns

Let us first define what a distributive lattice is. Note that they are defined differently in
[
PP05
]
; we take here as definition an equivalent condition, see Proposition 1.7.1 of
[
PP05
]
.

\vbox{}\left.\vbox{\vbox to36.15585pt{\vfil}}\right(\vbox{\vskip-7.5pt\begin{definition}For $V$ a vector space, a collection of its subspaces $U_{1},\cdots\hskip-0.1pt,U_{n}$ is said to be ``distributive'' (or ``generate a distributive lattice'') if there exists a basis of $V$ such that each $U_{i}$ is the span of a subset of this basis. Such a basis is called a ``distributing basis''.\end{definition}\vskip-7.5pt}

The significance of this definition is that it can be used to characterize Koszulness of a quadratic algebra.

\vbox{}\left.\vbox{\vbox to109.36115pt{\vfil}}\right[\vbox{\vskip-7.5pt\begin{proposition}A quadratic algebra $A$ with quadratic kernel $\mathfrak{q}$ is Koszul if and only if for every $n\geq 0$ the collection of subspaces
$$U_{i}=A_{1}^{\otimes i-1}\otimes\mathfrak{q}\otimes A_{1}^{\otimes n-i-1}\subset A_{1}^{\otimes n}$$for $1\leq i\leq n-1$ is distributive. More precisely, the following are equivalent:
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@$\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{A}{}_{j}(\mathbb{K},\mathbb{K})=0$ for all $i<j\leq n$;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@the Koszul complex is acyclic in positive internal degrees $\leq n$;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@the collection $\{U_{1},\cdots\hskip-0.1pt,U_{n-1}\}$ in $A_{1}^{\otimes n}$ is distributive.
\end{itemize}\end{proposition}\vskip-6.0pt}

Hence we seek a distributing basis of
B_{1}^{\otimes n}
for each
n\geq 0
, distributing the collection of spaces
B_{1}^{\otimes i-1}\otimes\mathfrak{p}\otimes B_{1}^{\otimes n-i-1}
. This is a massive space – there are infinitely many idempotents, and on top of that even for fixed idempotents the spaces are nongraded-infinite-dimensional, but there are some ‘divide-and-conquer’ reductions we can make to make this job more tractable.

As diagrams with different ending/beginning idempotents are linearly independent, it suffices to give a distributing basis for
e^{\theta-2n}B_{1}^{\otimes n}e^{\theta}
for each
\theta
.
Moreover, if we have a basis for
\theta=2n
, we can add propagating strings in appropriate places to all the basis diagrams to obtain a basis for any desired
\theta
; hence it suffices to give a distributing basis for
e^{0}B_{1}^{\otimes n}e^{2n}
. As diagrams with different numbers of total dots are linearly independent in
B
(thanks to the associated graded procedure), it also suffices to give a distributing basis for a fixed number of dots. By adding dots in appropriate places to basis diagrams, we can get any number of total dots we desire; hence it suffices to give a distributing basis for
e^{0}\mathring{B}_{1}^{\otimes n}e^{2n}
, where
\mathring{B}
is the algebra obtained from
B
by modding out by all diagrams with a positive number of dots:

\mathring{B}\coloneqq\bigoplus_{i}\text{F}_{0}{\text{N}\cal B}_{i}^{-}.

Most importantly, note that the spaces
B_{1}^{\otimes i-1}\otimes\mathfrak{p}\otimes B_{1}^{\otimes n-i-1}
respect these reductions, in the sense that each relation in
\mathfrak{p}
is homogeneous with respect to the beginning/ending idempotents, as well as with respect to the number of dots.

Things are already looking much more tractable, but we can make things even easier by looking at nesting patterns.

\vbox{}\left.\vbox{\vbox to96.1003pt{\vfil}}\right(\vbox{\vskip-7.5pt\begin{definition}The ``nesting pattern'' of a diagram is given by looking at its `shadow'. More precisely, ignore dots (if they exist), color each cap, and project the diagram to the horizontal axis; the result is a 1-dimensional topological subspace of the horizontal axis, consisting of segments of different colors, possibly overlapping, with their intersection inheriting both colors. Let these segments have a `thickness' coming from the number of colors they carry. Then forget the color, and the resulting 1-dimensional subspace with thickness, considered up to isotopy within the 1-dimensional parent space, is the nesting pattern of this diagram.\end{definition}\vskip-7.5pt}

Here’s a quick diagram depicting this:

Here to make the picture clearer we have staggered the shadows of the caps in drawing the nesting pattern; it does not matter which one goes on top; all that matters is where they overlap, if they do. It is clear that diagrams with different nesting patterns are linearly independent.
Also importantly, all relations in
\mathfrak{p}
are homogeneous with respect to nesting patterns.
Note that this is not true without passing from
{\text{N}\cal B}^{-}
to
B=\operatorname{gr}^{\text{F}}{\text{N}\cal B}^{-}
! Hence it suffices to provide a distributing basis of the subspace of
e^{0}\mathring{B}_{1}^{\otimes n}e^{2n}
consisting of a fixed nesting pattern
P
; denote this subspace
e^{0}\mathring{B}_{1}^{\otimes n}e^{2n}(\text{P})
.

P
. Also fix a numbering of each segment, in such a way that a segment labeled
j
nested inside a segment labeled
i
has
i<j
:

For any two segments, diagram
5
exhausts the possibilities of their nesting patterns.
Let each segment
i
have a formal algebraic symbol
x_{i}
attached to it. We think of
x_{i}
as ‘the cap for segment
i
’. Given a monomial in the
x_{i}
’s, we read it as right to left corresponding to bottom to top in nilBrauer diagrams. For example, if one has a pattern
P
of a segment labeled 2 sitting inside a segment labeled 1, then
x_{1}x_{2}
(cf.
x_{2}x_{1}
) corresponds to the following element of
e^{0}\mathring{B}_{1}^{\otimes 2}e^{4}
:

1

Note that
x_{2}x_{1}
is then an element in
\mathfrak{p}
. Similarly, if the pattern was two segments neither of which sits entirely inside the other, then
x_{1}x_{2}-x_{2}x_{1}
is an element in
\mathfrak{p}
. Here’s a slightly more complicated example:

\displaystyle x_{3}x_{2}x_{1}

\displaystyle=\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 32.40935pt\hbox{{\hbox{\kern-32.40935pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-32.40935pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\hbox to64.82pt{\vbox to64.82pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}
{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}}{{{}}{{}}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 36.91 22.15 C 51.35 22.15 62.7 13.94 66.44 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

{}
{}
{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 22.15 36.91 C 22.15 45.06 28.76 51.67 36.91 51.67 C 45.06 51.67 51.67 45.06 51.67 36.91}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}$}}}}}}}}}.

More generally, given any two segments
i,j
in
P
, corresponding to two caps
x_{i},x_{j}
, either

x_{i}x_{j}-x_{j}x_{i}\in\mathfrak{p}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{or}\hskip 10.00002ptx_{j}x_{i}\in\mathfrak{p},\>\;i<j;

the latter case corresponds to if segment
j
lies inside segment
i
, and the former is otherwise. Since the relation of ‘lying inside of’ is transitive, namely
k\subset j,\ j\subset i\implies k\subset i
, we have

x_{k}x_{j}\in\mathfrak{p},\ x_{j}x_{i}\in\mathfrak{p}\implies x_{k}x_{i}\in\mathfrak{p}.

Lastly, we only want each cap to appear once, so we must impose for each
i

x_{i}^{2}\in\mathfrak{p}.

We want a distributing basis for
e^{0}\mathring{B}_{1}^{\otimes n}e^{2n}(\text{P})
. There will be
n
segments in
P
, corresponding to caps
x_{1},\cdots\hskip-0.1pt,x_{n}
. Consider the algebra
A=A(\text{P})
over
\mathbb{C}
with generators
x_{1},\cdots\hskip-0.1pt,x_{n}
subject to the quadratic relations

(1)

x_{i}^{2}=0
for each
i
,

(2)

either
x_{i}x_{j}=x_{j}x_{i}
or
x_{j}x_{i}=0
for every
i<j
, depending on
P
; this will automatically be in such a way that
x_{k}x_{j}=0
and
x_{j}x_{i}=0\implies x_{k}x_{i}=0
;

clearly, if this were
n
-Koszul
5
5

5

We will note use the definition of
n
-Koszul in this paper, but for completeness let us recall it is defined as follows (see
[
PP05
, Chapter 2, Theorem 4.1]
): a quadratic algebra is
n
-Koszul if
\operatorname{Ext}_{A}^{i}{}_{j}(\mathbb{K},\mathbb{K})=0
for all
i<j\leq n
, or equivalently the collection
\{A_{1}^{\otimes i-1}\otimes\mathfrak{q}\otimes A_{1}^{\otimes n-i-1}\}_{1\leq i\leq n-1}
is distributive in
A_{1}^{\otimes n}
.
, then one has a basis of
A_{1}^{\otimes n}
distributing the collection
\{A_{1}^{\otimes i-1}\otimes\mathfrak{q}\otimes A_{1}^{\otimes n-i-1}\}_{i}
, and this same basis will be a distributing basis for
e^{0}\mathring{B}_{1}^{\otimes n}e^{2n}(\text{P})
under the obvious isomorphism. So we have reduced what we want to a (semi?)commutative algebra question. Thankfully it turns out such algebras
A(\text{P})
are not only
n
-Koszul but Koszul.

Reduction to Koszul theory

At this point we can cite a theorem from general Koszul theory.

\vbox{}\left.\vbox{\vbox to212.59816pt{\vfil}}\right[\vbox{\vskip-7.5pt\begin{theorem}Consider the quadratic algebra $A$ with generators $x_{1},\cdots\hskip-0.1pt,x_{n}$ subject to relations from the following list of (distinct) possibilities for each $i\neq j$:
\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@$x_{i}x_{j}=c_{ij}x_{j}x_{i}$ for $c_{ij}\in\mathbbm{k}^{\times}$;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@either $x_{i}x_{j}=0$ or $x_{j}x_{i}=0$ (but not both);
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@$x_{i}x_{j}=x_{j}x_{i}=0$;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@no relation;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@possibly $x_{i}^{2}=0$ or not for each $i$.
\end{enumerate}Let $\theta_{i}=x_{i}^{*}$ be the generators of $A^{!}$. Then $A$ is Koszul if and only if for any two nonzero monomials $x^{\alpha}\in A$ and $\theta^{\beta}\in A^{!}$, one can find $i$ and $\gamma$ such that either one of the following two is true (where $c\in\mathbbm{k}^{\times}$):
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@$x^{\alpha}=cx^{\gamma}x_{i}$ and $\theta_{i}\theta^{\beta}\neq 0$,
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@$x^{\alpha}x_{i}\neq 0$ and $c\theta_{i}\theta^{\gamma}=\theta^{\beta}$.
\end{itemize}\end{theorem}\vskip-6.0pt}

Using this, one can prove that for instance quotients of skew polynomial rings by monomial quadratic ideals are Koszul. The fact we will need is

\vbox{}\left.\vbox{\vbox to73.65698pt{\vfil}}\right[\vbox{\vskip-7.5pt\begin{proposition}Let $A$ be the algebra over $\mathbb{C}$ with generators $x_{1},\cdots\hskip-0.1pt,x_{n}$ subject to the relations
\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@$x_{i}^{2}=0$ for each $i$,
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@either $x_{i}x_{j}=x_{j}x_{i}$ or $x_{j}x_{i}=0$ for every $i<j$, in such a way that $x_{k}x_{j}=0$ and $x_{j}x_{i}=0\implies x_{k}x_{i}=0$.
\end{enumerate}Then $A$ is Koszul.\end{proposition}\vskip-6.0pt}

This proposition, in turn, is a special case of the following algebraic fact:

\vbox{}\left.\vbox{\vbox to123.42479pt{\vfil}}\right[\vbox{\vskip-7.5pt\begin{proposition}Consider an algebra $A$ which is generated by $x_{1},\cdots\hskip-0.1pt,x_{n}$ subject to the relations
\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@$x_{i}^{2}=0$ for each $i$;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@for each $i<j$ either:
\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@$x_{i}x_{j}=c_{ij}x_{j}x_{i}$ for $c_{ij}\in\mathbbm{k}^{\times}$, or
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@$x_{j}x_{i}=0$,
\end{enumerate}such that $x_{k}x_{j}=0,\ x_{j}x_{i}=0\implies x_{k}x_{i}=0$.
\end{enumerate}Then $A$ is Koszul.\end{proposition}\vskip-6.0pt}

Proof.

This is in essence a combinatorial argument. Recall the quadratic dual is
A^{!}=\raisebox{-1.49994pt}{\scalebox{1.32}[1.4]{$\nicefrac{{\otimes^{\bullet}A_{1}^{*}}}{{\mathfrak{q}^{\perp}}}$}}
; as such, inside
A^{!}
the relations satisfied by
\theta_{i}
are precisely

(1)

either
\theta_{i}\theta_{j}=-c_{ij}^{-1}\theta_{j}\theta_{i}
or
\theta_{i}\theta_{j}=0
for every
i<j
, in such a way that
\theta_{i}\theta_{j}=0
and
\theta_{j}\theta_{k}=0\implies\theta_{i}\theta_{k}=0
.

The relations
x_{i}^{2}=0
turn into no restrictions on nilpotence of
\theta_{i}
. Let us state for the record that the only nonzero possibilities for two variables to not commute are
\theta_{\text{big}}\theta_{\text{small}}
and
x_{\text{small}}x_{\text{big}}
.

We utilize the characterization of Froberg. Consider any two nonzero monomials
x^{\alpha}=(\cdots)x_{i}
and
\theta^{\beta}=\theta_{j}(\cdots)
. Write
(\cdots)x_{i}
on the left and
\theta_{j}(\cdots)
on the right:

x^{\alpha}=\underbrace{(\cdots)}_{x^{\alpha^{\prime}}}x_{i}\hskip 20.00003pt\theta_{j}\underbrace{(\cdots)}_{\theta^{\beta^{\prime}}}=\theta^{\beta};

we wish to show we can push one of
x_{i}
or
\theta_{j}
to the other side and still have two nonzero monomials.

A quick lemma:

\vbox{}\left.\vbox{\vbox to28.70715pt{\vfil}}\right[\vbox{\vskip-7.5pt\begin{lemma}In the above setup, in $(x^{\alpha^{\prime}})x_{i}\neq 0$, if $x_{j}$ is such that $x_{j}x_{i}=0$, then $x_{j}$ cannot be a factor in $x^{\alpha^{\prime}}$. Dually, in $\theta_{j}(\theta^{\beta^{\prime}})\neq 0$, if $\theta_{k}$ is such that $\theta_{j}\theta_{k}=0$, then $\theta_{k}$ cannot be a factor in $\theta^{\beta^{\prime}}$.\end{lemma}\vskip-6.0pt}

Let us quickly see this. We argue only for the former case, as the other argument is dual. Suppose there was a
x_{j}
in
x^{\alpha^{\prime}}
; let us try to commute it to the right. If it can be commuted rightwards (using relations of form
x_{a}x_{b}=c_{ab}x_{b}x_{a}
) to meet
x_{i}
, then one has
x_{j}x_{i}
as a subexpression (up to a nonzero constant) and this monomial must be zero. If it cannot be commuted to meet
x_{i}
, it must be that it were stuck somewhere along the way as
x_{j}x_{k}
for
j<k
; but for this to be the case, it must be that
x_{k}x_{j}=0
, which together with
x_{j}x_{i}=0
implies
x_{k}x_{i}=0
. Now
x_{k}
is closer to
x_{i}
than
x_{j}
, and we can again try to commute it towards
x_{i}
. By induction on the distance from
x_{i}
, this shows the monomial must be zero.

Now we return to trying to push one of
x_{i}
or
\theta_{j}
to the other side. We break into cases.

Case 1.
Suppose
\theta_{i}\theta_{j}=0
,
i<j
, which is to say that
x_{j}x_{i}=0
. Then split
\theta_{j}
off from
\theta^{\beta}
and move it to
x^{\alpha}
; note
x_{i}x_{j}\neq 0
. In order to show
x^{\alpha}x_{j}=x^{\alpha^{\prime}}x_{i}x_{j}\neq 0
, we would need to show the introduction of
x_{j}
does not allow for any new cancellation, namely that (a) it cannot be the case that there is a
x_{j}
in
x^{\alpha^{\prime}}
that can be commuted to meet the new
x_{j}
and (b) it cannot be the case that there is a
x_{k}
such that
x_{k}x_{j}=0
in
x^{\alpha^{\prime}}
that can be commuted to meet the new
x_{j}
. The former possibility is impossible due to the lemma above. The latter case is also not possible because
x_{k}x_{j}=0
and
x_{j}x_{i}=0
gives
x_{k}x_{i}=0
, and again by the lemma such a
x_{k}
cannot be a factor in
x^{\alpha^{\prime}}
.

Case 2.
Suppose
\theta_{i}\theta_{j}=-c_{ij}^{-1}\theta_{j}\theta_{i}
, and that there is no
\theta_{k}
in
\theta^{\beta^{\prime}}
such that
\theta_{i}\theta_{k}=0
. Then we can simply split
x_{i}
from
x^{\alpha}
and move it to
\theta^{\beta}
, and there is no possibility of cancellation.

Case 3.
Suppose
\theta_{i}\theta_{j}=-c_{ij}^{-1}\theta_{j}\theta_{i}
, but there is a
\theta_{k}
in
\theta^{\beta^{\prime}}
such that
\theta_{i}\theta_{k}=0
. Again, by trying to commute
\theta_{k}
towards
\theta_{j}
(using relations like
\theta_{a}\theta_{b}=-c_{ab}^{-1}\theta_{b}\theta_{a}
) and inducting on the distance from
\theta_{j}
, we can get to the point
\theta^{\beta}=c\theta_{j}\theta_{k^{\prime}}(\cdots)
, where
k^{\prime}
might be different from
k
but still satisfies
\theta_{i}\theta_{k^{\prime}}=0
(by using the procedure as in the proof of the lemma). But
\theta_{j},\theta_{k^{\prime}}
must commute (up to
-c_{k^{\prime}j}^{-1}
), for otherwise we would have
\theta_{k^{\prime}}\theta_{j}=0
, which would imply
\theta_{i}\theta_{j}=0
, contradiction. Hence we can write
\theta^{\beta}=c^{\prime}\theta_{k^{\prime}}\theta_{j}(\cdots)
, where
\theta_{i}\theta_{k^{\prime}}=0
; this reduces to Case 1.

These three cases exhaust all possibilities, and we are done.∎

Remark
.

It is remarkable that this fact about the nilBrauer algebra ends up boiling down to a (non?)commutative algebra fact! Maybe worth remarking is that this is almost not true. Indeed, according to this proposition,

\nicefrac{{\mathbb{C}\langle x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}\rangle}}{{x_{i}^{2}=0,\ x_{1}x_{3}=x_{3}x_{1},\ x_{2}x_{3}=x_{3}x_{2},\ x_{2}x_{1}=0}}

is Koszul, but the algebra

\nicefrac{{\mathbb{C}\langle x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}\rangle}}{{x_{1}x_{3}=x_{3}x_{1},\ x_{1}x_{2}=x_{2}x_{1}=0}}

is not, as remarked by
[
PP05
]
on page 91.

Also, note that the above proof still works even if you weaken the
x_{i}^{2}=0
for each
i
to
x_{i}^{2}=0
for some
i
. We don’t need this though.

6.
Standard Ext Groups, Part 3: Kostantness and BGG (Main Results)

Kostantness

Having established Koszulness, we can now show

\vbox{}\left.\vbox{\vbox to15.70143pt{\vfil}}\right[\vbox{\vskip-7.5pt\begin{theorem}At $t=0$, the Ext groups $\operatorname{Ext}^{\bullet}_{\text{N}\cal B}({\text{N}\cal B}^{\geq\theta}e^{\theta},L_{0})$ are concentrated in one degree (namely $n=\theta/2$); i.e., $L_{0}$ is a Kostant module.\end{theorem}\vskip-6.0pt}

Proof.

Indeed, we can compute these Ext groups
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}\text{Hom}}_{\text{N}\cal B}({\text{N}\cal B}^{\geq\theta}e^{\theta},\mathbb{C})=\operatorname{\mathsf{R}\text{Hom}}_{A^{-}}(\mathbbm{k}e^{\theta},\mathbb{C})
using the resolution of
\mathbbm{k}e^{\theta}
given above. So

\displaystyle\operatorname{Ext}^{\bullet}_{\text{N}\cal B}({\text{N}\cal B}^{\geq\theta}e^{\theta},\mathbb{C}e^{0})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Ext}^{\bullet}_{A^{-}}(\mathbbm{k}e^{\theta},\mathbb{C}e^{0})

\displaystyle=H^{\bullet}\Hom_{A^{-}}(A^{-}\otimes I^{\otimes\bullet}e^{\theta},\mathbb{C}e^{0})

\displaystyle=H^{\bullet}\Hom_{\mathbb{K}}(I^{\otimes\bullet}e^{\theta},\mathbb{C}e^{0})

\displaystyle=H^{\bullet}\Hom_{\mathbb{C}}(e^{0}I^{\otimes\bullet}e^{\theta},\mathbb{C}e^{0})

\displaystyle=H^{-\bullet}e^{0}I^{\otimes\bullet}e^{\theta}.

Here the complex
e^{0}I^{\otimes\bullet}e^{\theta}
has differential coming from the original complex, namely
\partial_{k}\colon a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{k}\longmapsto\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}(-1)^{i}a_{1}\otimes\cdots a_{i}a_{i+1}\cdots\otimes a_{k}
. Note that for instance
e^{0}I^{\otimes 0}e^{\theta}=e^{0}\mathbb{K}e^{\theta}=0
if
\theta\neq 0
(the
\theta=0
case is trivial anyway since
\Delta_{0}
is projective).

However, as we know
{\text{N}\cal B}^{-}
is Koszul, we immediately know that
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}\text{Hom}}_{{\text{N}\cal B}^{-}}(\mathbb{K},\mathbb{K})
is diagonal, meaning that it is only nonzero when the homological degree matches with the cap degree. As we’ve mentioned earlier,
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}\text{Hom}}_{{\text{N}\cal B}^{-}}(\mathbb{K},\mathbb{K})
has a left and right
\mathbb{K}
-action corresponding to picking out the weights in the first and second entries, and by picking out the weight space
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}\text{Hom}}_{{\text{N}\cal B}^{-}}(\mathbbm{k}e^{\theta},\mathbbm{k}e^{0})
, we see that the cap degree is concentrated in
n=\theta/2
, so that necessarily the homological degree must also be concentrated in
n
.∎

In fact, we can compute the dimensions of these Ext groups. Before doing so, some setup: Brundan had introduced
T_{f,n}(q)
as the
q
-generating function of the number of tethered chord diagrams with
f
free chords and
n
tethered chords. Rather than use this notation, we will let

Y_{i,j}\text{ be the generating function for dotless $\text{Y}$ diagrams from $j$ to $i$}.

The relation between the two is
T_{f,n}=Y_{n,2f+n}
and
Y_{i,j}=T_{\frac{j-i}{2},i}
. BWW showed that these generating functions satisfy
T_{f,n}=T_{f,n-1}+q^{n}[n+1]T_{f-1,n+1}
, though we will not appeal to this.

Remark
.

Another way to deduce the following dimension claim is to use the linear independence of the symbols
[\overline{\Delta}_{\lambda}]
in the Grothendieck group of the nilBrauer modules, in conjunction with the BWW character formula

[L_{0}]=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{n}\frac{q^{-n}}{(1-q^{-4})(1-q^{-8})\cdots(1-q^{-4n})}[\overline{\Delta}_{2n}].

Then, by the spectral sequence in Theorem
applied to
L_{0}
, the alternating sums of the characters of the terms of the first page must be the same as the character of
L_{0}
, but by Kostantness this alternating sum is simply the alternating sum of the standard Ext groups across
n=\theta/2
, so that we know

[L_{0}]=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{n}[\jmath^{2n}_{!}\operatorname{Ext}^{n}_{\text{N}\cal B}(\Delta(2n),L_{0})^{*}]=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{n}\frac{1}{[2n]!}\dim(\operatorname{Ext}^{n}_{\text{N}\cal B}(\Delta(2n),L_{0})^{*})[\overline{\Delta}_{2n}],

where we recall that
\operatorname{Ext}^{n}_{\text{N}\cal B}(\Delta(2n),M)^{*}
is a
{\text{N}\cal B}^{2n}
-module and note
\jmath^{2n}_{!}L_{2n}(2n)=\overline{\Delta}_{2n}
and
\dim L_{2n}(2n)=[2n]!
. Comparison with the BWW formula gives an alternative way to deduce the following.

\vbox{}\left.\vbox{\vbox to38.15585pt{\vfil}}\right[\vbox{\vskip-7.5pt\begin{theorem}Let $t=0$ and $\theta=2n$. Then the dimension of the Ext groups are exactly what one expects for the BGG resolution to hold, namely
$$\dim_{q}\operatorname{Ext}^{n}_{\text{N}\cal B}({\text{N}\cal B}^{\geq\theta}e^{\theta},L_{0})^{*}=\frac{q^{-n}[2n]!}{(1-q^{-4})\cdots(1-q^{-4n})}=\frac{(1+q^{2}+q^{4})\cdots(1+\cdots+q^{4n-4})}{(1-q^{-2})^{n}}.$$For convenience in the future we will denote $m_{\theta}(q)=\frac{q^{-n}}{(1-q^{-4})\cdots(1-q^{-4n})}$.\end{theorem}\vskip-6.0pt}

Proof.

As the complex (here the heart symbol indicates homological degree 0)

0\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow e^{0}I^{\otimes n}e^{\theta}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\cdots\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow e^{0}Ie^{\theta}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\overset{\heartsuit}{e^{0}\mathbb{K}e^{\theta}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow 0

is quasiisomorphic to
\operatorname{Ext}^{\bullet}_{\text{N}\cal B}({\text{N}\cal B}^{\geq\theta}e^{\theta},L_{0})^{*}=\operatorname{Ext}^{n}_{\text{N}\cal B}({\text{N}\cal B}^{\geq\theta}e^{\theta},L_{0})^{*}[n]
, one has

\sum_{k=0}^{n}(-1)^{k}\dim_{q}e^{0}I^{\otimes k}e^{\theta}=(-1)^{n}\dim_{q}\operatorname{Ext}^{n}_{\text{N}\cal B}({\text{N}\cal B}^{\geq\theta}e^{\theta},L_{0})^{*},

where

\dim_{q}e^{0}I^{\otimes k}e^{2n}=\frac{1}{(1-q^{-2})^{n}}\sum_{1\leq i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k-1}\leq n-1}Y_{0,2i_{1}}\cdots Y_{2i_{k-1},2n};

the factor in front is for the dots (recall propagating strands cannot have dots for
Y
).

So the claim is then saying that

\sum_{k=0}^{n}(-1)^{k}\frac{1}{(1-q^{-2})^{n}}\sum_{1\leq i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k-1}\leq n-1}Y_{0,2i_{1}}\cdots Y_{2i_{k-1},2n}=(-1)^{n}\frac{q^{-n}[2n]!}{(1-q^{-4})\cdots(1-q^{-4n})}.

A straight-forward computation shows that

\displaystyle(1-q^{-2})^{n}\frac{[2n]!}{(1-q^{-4})\cdots(1-q^{-4n})}

\displaystyle=\frac{[2n]!}{(1+q^{-2})(1+q^{-2}+q^{-4}+q^{-6})\cdots(1+\cdots+q^{-4n+2})}

\displaystyle=\frac{q^{-n}[2n]!}{(1-q^{-4})\cdots(1-q^{-4n})}\cdot\frac{q^{1+2+\cdots+(2n-1)}}{q^{2+6+\cdots+(4n-2)}}\cdot\frac{q^{1+2+\cdots+(2n-1)}}{q^{4+8+\cdots+(4n-4)}}

\displaystyle=q^{n}\frac{(1+q^{2})(1+q^{2}+q^{4})\cdots(1+\cdots+q^{4n-2})}{(1+q^{2})(1+\cdots+q^{6})\cdots(1+\cdots+q^{4n-2})}

\displaystyle=q^{n}(1+q^{2}+q^{4})(1+q^{2}+q^{4}+q^{6}+q^{8})\cdots(1+\cdots+q^{4n-4}),

so that the above claim is equivalent to saying (note the
k=0
term is trivial)

(1+q^{2}+q^{4})(1+q^{2}+q^{4}+q^{6}+q^{8})\cdots(1+\cdots+q^{4n-4})=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\sum_{1\leq i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k-1}\leq n-1}(-1)^{n-k}Y_{0,2i_{1}}\cdots Y_{2i_{k-1},2n}.

This is morally a PIE (principle of inclusion-exclusion) argument. On the LHS is the generating function for number of ways to go from
2n
strings to 0 strings in
n
steps, where each step has exactly one cap whose left-most vertex is at the far left (or alternatively whose right-most vertex is at the far right). On the RHS one starts at
k=n
with the product of all the ways to go from
2n
strings to 0 strings in
n
steps except with no requirement on where the endpoints of the cap are, and each subsequent term consists of ways of combining multiple steps into one. To make this identity easier to show, we can apply a reduction using the idea of nesting patterns from earlier: it is clear that for each nesting pattern, there is exactly one cap diagram counted by the LHS, namely the one for which the caps are arranged like a staircase from left to right. Then it suffices to show this for a fixed nesting pattern, as the terms on the RHS can also be organized according to nesting patterns. WLOG it suffices to show this for the nesting pattern of
n
disjoint segments, where the desired identity is

1=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{1\leq i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k-1}\leq n-1}(-1)^{n-k}\binom{i_{1}}{0}\cdots\binom{n}{i_{k-1}}.

We can now symbolically manipulate

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{1\leq i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k-1}\leq n-1}(-1)^{n-k}\binom{i_{1}}{0}\cdots\binom{n}{i_{k-1}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1}+\cdots+j_{k}=n\\
j_{i}>0\end{subarray}}(-1)^{n-k}\binom{n}{j_{1},\cdots\hskip-0.1pt,j_{k}}

\displaystyle\text{(PIE)}\implies

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}(-1)^{n-k}\big(k^{n}-\binom{k}{1}(k-1)^{n}+\binom{k}{2}(k-2)^{n}-\cdots\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}(-1)^{n-k}\sum_{i=0}^{k}(-1)^{k-i}\binom{k}{i}i^{n}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{n-i}\binom{k}{i}i^{n}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{n-i}i^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\binom{k}{i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{n}(-1)^{n-i}\binom{n+1}{i+1}i^{n}.

So we wish to show
\sum_{i=0}^{n}(-1)^{n-i}\binom{n+1}{i+1}i^{n}=1
. In fact, we claim that

\sum_{i=0}^{n}(-1)^{n-i}\binom{n+1}{i+1}i^{k}=(-1)^{n-k}\hskip 20.00003pt\forall\;k\leq n.

This follows by applying the following lemma to
P(i)=(i-1)^{k}
and using
n+1
as the top entry in the binomial coefficient. Indeed, then one has

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\sum_{i+1=0}^{n+1}(-1)^{i+1}\binom{n+1}{i+1}P(i+1)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=-1}^{n}(-1)^{n-i}\binom{n+1}{i+1}i^{k}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{n+1}\binom{n+1}{0}(-1)^{k}+\sum_{i=0}^{n}(-1)^{n-i}\binom{n+1}{i+1}i^{k}

\displaystyle\implies(-1)^{n-k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{n}(-1)^{n-i}\binom{n+1}{i+1}i^{k},

as claimed.∎

\vbox{}\left.\vbox{\vbox to27.48232pt{\vfil}}\right[\vbox{\vskip-7.5pt\begin{lemma}For any polynomial $\deg P<n$, one has
$$\sum_{i=0}^{n}(-1)^{i}\binom{n}{i}P(i)=0.$$\end{lemma}\vskip-6.0pt}

Proof.

One can prove this using generating function theory. Indeed, consider

\sum_{i=0}^{n}\binom{n}{i}P(i)x^{i};

we wish to compute this for
x=-1
. By general theory, this is

\sum_{i=0}^{n}\binom{n}{i}P(i)x^{i}=P(x\partial)(1+x)^{n}.

The claim is that

(x\partial)^{k}(1+x)^{n}\rvert_{x=-1}=0\hskip 20.00003pt\forall\;k<n,

so that by linearity we are done. But this is true by induction: it is evident that each
(x\partial)^{\text{some power}}(1+x)^{n}
will look like linear combinations of terms of form
x^{i}(1+x)^{j}
. Note

(x\partial)(x^{i}(1+x)^{j})=ix^{i}(1+x)^{j}+jx^{i+1}(1+x)^{j-1}.

In particular, if
x\partial
is applied less than
n
times, the exponent on
(1+x)
will never be zero, so when
x
is set to
-1
all these terms vanish. This concludes the proof.

Another method, much cleaner, is due to Matthew Hase-Liu, using the philosophy of finite differences. Using that the binomial coefficients
\binom{x}{i}
for
0\leq i\leq n
form a basis for the space of polynomials of degree at most
n
, it suffices to show the claim for
P(i)=\binom{i}{k}
. However,

\sum_{i}(-1)^{i}\binom{n}{i}\binom{i}{k}=\sum_{i}(-1)^{i}\binom{n-k}{n-i}\binom{n}{k}=\binom{n}{k}\sum_{i}(-1)^{i}\binom{n-k}{n-i}=0,

so we are done.∎

Recall that the standard Ext groups
\operatorname{Ext}_{\text{N}\cal B}^{n}({\text{N}\cal B}^{\geq\theta}e^{\theta},L_{0})^{*}
act as multiplicity spaces for the BGG resolution in a sense. Unfortunately, these standard Ext groups are not semisimple as
{\text{N}\cal B}^{\theta}
-modules; the next best thing we can do to study the structure of these Ext groups, and by extension the structure of the terms appearing in the BGG resolution, is to write down a Jordan-Holder filtration for it, with descriptions of its quotients. We give a Loewy filtration (in the sense that quotients are semisimple) below.

\vbox{}\left.\vbox{\vbox to111.70143pt{\vfil}}\right[\vbox{\vskip-7.5pt\begin{theorem}At $t=0$, letting $\theta=2n$, the ${\text{N}\cal B}^{\theta}$-module $\operatorname{Ext}_{\text{N}\cal B}^{n}({\text{N}\cal B}^{\geq\theta}e^{\theta},L_{0})^{*}$ is isomorphic to a quotient of the polynomial ring
$$\operatorname{Ext}_{\text{N}\cal B}^{n}({\text{N}\cal B}^{\geq\theta}e^{\theta},L_{0})^{*}\cong\raisebox{-1.49994pt}{\scalebox{1.32}[1.4]{$\nicefrac{{\mathbb{C}[X_{1},\cdots\hskip-0.1pt,X_{\theta}]}}{{\langle\textsl{p}_{1},\textsl{p}_{3},\cdots\hskip-0.1pt,\textsl{p}_{2n-1}\rangle}}$}},$$where $\textsl{p}_{i}$ is the $i$-th power sum in the dots $X$, and has a filtration $\operatorname{Ext}_{\text{N}\cal B}^{n}({\text{N}\cal B}^{\geq\theta}e^{\theta},L_{0})^{*}=\text{F}^{0}_{\operatorname{Ext}}\supset\text{F}^{1}_{\operatorname{Ext}}\supset\cdots$ such that the quotients are
$$\operatorname{gr}^{k}\operatorname{Ext}_{\text{N}\cal B}^{n}({\text{N}\cal B}^{\geq\theta}e^{\theta},L_{0})^{*}=L_{\theta}(\theta)\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}q^{-n}\mathbb{C}[\textsl{p}_{2},\textsl{p}_{4},\cdots\hskip-0.1pt,\textsl{p}_{2n}]_{\deg_{\text{sym}}=k}=L_{\theta}(\theta)\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}q^{-n}E_{\theta,k},$$where $E_{\theta,k}\coloneqq\mathbb{C}[\textsl{p}_{2},\cdots\hskip-0.1pt,\textsl{p}_{2n}]_{\deg_{\text{sym}}=k}$ denotes the subspace of $\mathbb{C}[\textsl{p}_{2},\cdots\hskip-0.1pt,\textsl{p}_{2n}]$ consisting of symmetric-degree-$k$ elements, where the symmetric degree is defined by $\deg_{\text{sym}}\textsl{p}_{i}=1$ (contrast this with the quantum degree, which has $\deg\textsl{p}_{i}=2i$).\end{theorem}\vskip-6.0pt}

Proof.

To prove this, we utilize the nilBrauer action on the Ext group as realized by
H^{n}_{\flat}({\text{N}\cal B}^{-}:L_{0})^{*}
. Note that as
L_{0}=\mathbb{C}e^{0}
, we can ignore the
\Hom_{\mathbb{K}}({\mathop{\square}},L_{0})^{*}
and calculate the Ext groups directly as the cohomology of
e^{0}(I^{\flat})^{\otimes_{A^{\natural}}\bullet}e^{\theta}
. Under this identification, the Cartan nilHeckes act on the right, by attaching diagrams below.

We have already computed the dimension of
\operatorname{Ext}^{n,*}=\operatorname{Ext}_{\text{N}\cal B}^{n}({\text{N}\cal B}^{\geq\theta}e^{\theta},L_{0})^{*}
above. Consider the element of lowest quantum degree, corresponding to the term
q^{4+8+\cdots+4n-4}=q^{4\binom{n}{2}}
in the quantum dimension. This element is constructed as (we remind the reader the orange lines in the middle stand for tensor symbols)

v^{+}_{\theta}=v^{+}=\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 69.75359pt\hbox{{\hbox{\kern-69.75359pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-69.7536pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\hbox to139.51pt{\vbox to139.51pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

Note that, considered as an element in the complex computing
H^{\bullet}_{\flat}
(so that the tensor is over
{\text{N}\cal B}^{\natural}
and therefore allows crossings to pass through), this is the same as the non-intersecting nest of loops on top of two copies of the longest permutation on
n
strands, denoted
w_{0}*w_{0}
:

Indeed, since the multiplication of any two adjacent tensor factors is zero, this term is clearly in the kernel of the complex, and by using the
I^{\otimes\bullet}
incarnation of the nilcohomology one sees this vector is nonzero in the Ext group (indeed there is no
n+1
term and so there is nothing to mod out by). By using the nested loops on longest permutations description, it is clear that any crossing will kill this vector, i.e.
(v^{+})\cdot\sigma=0
(note the right action notation), which is why we called it
v^{+}
. Recall from
[
BWW23a
, Theorem 3.8/Corollary 3.9]
that the Schur
q
-functions
\textsl{q}_{\text{odd}}
and the odd power sums in dots
\textsl{p}_{\text{odd}}
are in the center of nilBrauer. Since the odd power sum in zero variables is zero, this immediately shows that
(v^{+})\cdot\textsl{p}_{\text{odd}}=0
. While the odd
q
-functions do not become zero at the zeroth idempotent, by commuting one of them to the top of a diagram, one can witness the entire thing as an image under the differential; hence any diagram with at least one
q
-function is zero in the cohomology. Hence
\textsl{q}_{\text{odd}}
also act by zero on
v^{+}
,
(v^{+})\cdot\textsl{q}_{\text{odd}}=0
.

Remark: as, under the usual grading conventions, the highest weight vector of
L_{\theta}(\theta)
should have degree
-\binom{\theta}{2}
, the vector
v^{+}
which is of degree
-4\binom{n}{2}=-\binom{\theta}{2}+n
would generate a simple (after passing to the head) shifted upwards by
n
.

Now consider the vectors
(v^{+})\cdot\textsl{p}_{2i}
(note the right action notation). By writing
v^{+}
as the nest of loops on
w_{0}*w_{0}
, we can commute
\textsl{p}_{2i}
(being in the center of nilHecke) past the crossings, and it is visibly evident that
\textsl{p}_{\text{even}}
acting on the nest of loops is nonzero, e.g.
